Hollywood’s Top 25 Legal Troubleshooters

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Criminal charges, custody fights, contested fortunes — meet the lawyers who handle the problems agents can’t negotiate away

In Hollywood, having a lawyer on speed dial can mean only one of two things. A deal is imminent, or a jury is deliberating. If it’s the latter, chances are the attorney on the other end of the line is on this list.

The 25 Legal Troubleshooters on these pages handle the problems agents can’t negotiate away: criminal charges, custody fights, contested fortunes and who gets to sell the Lakers. Their firms represent stars, moguls and heirs, although lately the L.A. Public Defender’s Office has been giving them unexpected competition. The lawyers themselves are attracting attention, too. Brian Steel has a Drake song named after him; Laura Wasser has a fashion campaign. Others spend their days explaining why a chatbot shouldn’t rewrite your trust.

All are worth keeping in your contacts. Just in case.

This firmspent the year representing famous clients facing charges of murder, rape and sex trafficking. Agnifilo and Geragos followed their defense of Sean “Diddy” Combs with Harvey Weinstein’s third trial, which ended in a hung jury, while another client, Oren Alexander, Kim Kardashian’s former real estate broker, was convicted of sex trafficking alongside brothers Tal and Alon. (Tal was represented by Deanna Paul, now a partner at Geragos & Geragos.) Friedman Agnifilo, meanwhile, helped take the death penalty off the table for Luigi Mangione, then pressed to dismiss his state murder case on double jeopardy grounds following his federal plea. But the firm’s most incendiary client this year has nothing to do with Hollywood: Winston Lee is one of seven former Cornell fraternity members accused in a civil suit of drugging and gang-raping a fellow student.

Actor Olivier Martinez had Baghdaserians in his corner during his headline-grabbing custody battle with ex-wife Halle Berry. In October, the lawyer secured a temporary restraining order and primary physical custody of their 13-year-old son. The judge later dissolved the restraining order but kept Martinez the primary custodial parent. Martinez is now seeking increased child support and $240,000 in legal fees from Berry. Another custody client: Basketball Wives’ Clayanna Warthen, whose dispute involved former NBA player Andre Iguodala.

Berk’s clients sit on both sides of criminal cases: She has represented stalking victim Jennifer Aniston and defended Harvey Weinstein. She says three-quarters of her practice involves preventing charges from being filed. But singer D4vd’s murder case was impossible to keep quiet. Berk defended him against charges of killing 14-year-old Celeste Rivas Hernandez before withdrawing Aug. 31, when he qualified for a public defender — a growing trend in Hollywood (see Alan Jackson).

Two music cases, two different codas. Bloom’s firm settled Lauren Pisciotta’s harassment and assault suit against Kanye West in July, ending a two-year fight. But former Danity Kane singer Dawn Richard’s suit accusing Sean “Diddy” Combs of sexual assault and physical abuse was dismissed because most claims were filed too late. Bloom’s targets also include the FBI: Eight Jeffrey Epstein survivors she represents sued the bureau for allegedly failing to investigate reports of his abuse.

After winning “Blurred Lines” for Marvin Gaye’s family, Busch is taking on artificial intelligence. In October, he sued Anthropic on behalf of Elvis Presley’s rights holders, including Authentic Brands Group, alleging its Claude models trained on 274 Presley songs without permission. The demand: up to $41.1 million — or about $150,000 per song. Anthropic disputes the claims. Similar suits for music publishers Reservoir Media and Round Hill have made Busch a recurring adversary for the AI company.

More than 10 years after his death, Muhammad Ali is still one of Hollywood’s most active clients, and Eisen is the lawyer guarding his name. In January, the U.S. Postal Service issued an Ali Forever stamp, a milestone for an estate that the firm’s trusts and estates co-chair has helped steer through years of name-and-likeness deals. His firm’s other clients: Marilyn Monroe, Elvis Presley and Albert Einstein.

Findling and co-counsel Brian Steel dismantled the government’s murder-for-hire case against rapper Lil Durk, winning acquittals on all five counts. Findling argued that Durk’s former assistant, testifying for the prosecution, was himself the “mastermind,” urging jurors to see a “tsunami of reasonable doubt.” Afterward, 50 Cent dubbed him the “Billion Dollar Lawyer.” His client list also includes Cardi B and President Donald Trump.

Liner Freedman; Meister Seelig & Schuster PLLC

Freedman wielded the machete and Schuster the scalpel for actor-director Justin Baldoni in his battle with It Ends With Us co-star Blake Lively. His $400 million countersuit failed, but her harassment claims were dismissed, and the two sides settled without a payout. Lively later won $407,000 for defending against Baldoni’s defamation claim — just 5 percent of her $8 million legal-fee request, which Freedman called a “significant victory.”

The lawyer for accusers of Harvey Weinstein and Sean “Diddy” Combs is now representing the woman at the center of the Cornell fraternity rape case. Giuffra’s suit alleges seven fraternity members drugged and gang-raped her and that Cornell failed her. As the case exploded online, his client faced threats, doxing and swatting, while supporters rallied under #IAmJaneDoe to protect her identity. New York Attorney General Letitia James is overseeing a criminal investigation.

An O.J. Simpson “Dream Team” alum, Holley represented pitcher Tyler Skaggs’ family in its wrongful death case against the Angels, arguing the team failed to stop an employee supplying players with drugs. The club settled during jury deliberations. Alongside Mangels, she also helped record label Del Records cut a proposed federal fine — for doing business with a sanctioned, cartel-linked concert promoter — from $6.7 million to $1.8 million. Restraining orders are also a specialty; the two have secured them for Cindy Crawford, Kaia Gerber and Lana Del Rey.

When gamers take their grievances to court, Kumar represents the companies behind the screens. She reps Microsoft and Mojang, creator of Minecraft, in suits accusing game companies of hooking children. Her clients also include Tinder and Hinge, for which she secured dismissals in two cases alleging the apps failed to remove user profiles and messages. She also helped technology trade group NetChoice keep key provisions of California’s age-appropriate online design law blocked on appeal.

Jackson, who has defended Harvey Weinstein and Kevin Spacey, exited as Nick Reiner’s attorney in January. That notorious patricide-and-matricide case is now being handled by what’s rapidly turning into Hollywood’s hottest new legal shop — the L.A. County Public Defender’s Office. Not only is public defender Kimberly Greene now representing Reiner, but her colleagues Walid Kandeel and Lisa Roth took over singer D4vd’s murder defense from Blair Berk in August. Jackson, meanwhile, is teaming with former client Karen Read for a true-crime podcast, Read Between the Lines, debuting in early 2027.

Hudson and Moses didn’t just get Justin Baldoni’s $400 million countersuit against Blake Lively and Ryan Reynolds thrown out; they helped stick him with $407,000 of Lively’s legal bill, securing the first federal court award under California’s law protecting sexual harassment accusers from retaliatory defamation suits. Moses helped draft the motion; Hudson led Lively’s team. Lively’s own claims ended in a settlement without a payout.

Nikas won $102.2 million for the Morgan Art Foundation against publisher Michael McKenzie over unauthorized and altered works based on Robert Indiana’s LOVE — the familiar sculpture with the tilted O. He calls it the largest art-litigation verdict on record. His partner Spiro had more mixed results with client Elon Musk: Delaware’s Supreme Court reinstated his roughly $56 billion Tesla pay package, but a San Francisco jury found he misled Twitter investors.

Some brothers fared better than others. Paul defended celebrity realtor Tal Alexander against charges that he and his siblings drugged and sexually trafficked women. The jury convicted all three, and each received a 30-year sentence. Geragos had more luck with the Lyle and Erik Menendez, helping them secure a new parole hearing roughly 17 months ahead of schedule after the brothers were denied release last year. His TMZ appearances stirred up some controversy, though, with Geragos’ comments on the Cornell fraternity rape case prompted the accuser’s lawyer to allege that he was effectively assisting the defense team, which includes his daughter, Teny, at Agnifilo Intrater. Geragos denies it.

He doesn’t represent Hollywood actors or battle for studios, but the 75-year-old Massachusetts attorney did become one of 2026’s biggest reality stars. His livestreamed defense of Lindsay Clancy, accused of killing her three children, inspired more than 16,500 TikTok videos under his hashtag, autograph requests outside court and fan-made “Better Call Kevin” merch. Reddington argued Clancy was not criminally responsible due to postpartum psychosis. The jury deadlocked in September.

After helping Britney Spears escape her conservatorship, Rosengart fought for FKA twigs to reclaim her voice, winning an agreement lifting restrictions on discussing her sexual abuse allegations against ex-boyfriend Shia LaBeouf. He’s also defending Selena Gomez against investors who accuse her of failing to deliver on commitments to build and promote her mental health startup, Wondermind.

Royce has handled splits for Arnold Schwarzenegger, Channing Tatum and Forest Whitaker. Now she’s representing Lori Loughlin, whose marriage to Mossimo Giannulli survived the college admissions scandal and prison terms for both but ended in separation after nearly 28 years of marriage, with Loughlin filing for divorce in September.

A letter from Marty Singer has ruined many a Hollywood morning. In November, he accused filmmaker Yi Zhou of harassing Jeremy Renner after she alleged the actor sent explicit messages and threatened to call ICE. He also represented Priscilla Presley against former business partners who accused her of concealing a $25 million jewelry collection; they dropped that suit in February, retaining the right to refile. In March, Lavely & Singer became Singer Weinsten Wolf & Jonelis, adding three longtime partners to the firm’s name.

Most lawyers hope for a referral. Steel got a Drake song named after him. After representing Young Thug and joining Sean “Diddy” Combs’ defense, the Atlanta attorney teamed with Drew Findling to win Lil Durk acquittals on all five counts in his federal murder-for-hire trial. Fans swarmed him outside court after the verdict. Durk remains jailed ahead of a separate racketeering trial in August 2027.

Strauss and Forster help Hollywood’s wealthy pass fortunes to the next generation, minimizing estate taxes and planning for the transfer of family businesses. Lately, some clients have acquired another adviser: a chatbot. Strauss told CNBC that clients are uploading trust documents to AI, then returning with suggested revisions that can take hours to review. “I have not actually received a single workable suggestion from that process,” he says. The firm is updating its contracts to warn that sharing confidential documents with AI “lawyers” could jeopardize attorney-client privilege.

After helping secure Lachlan Murdoch’s control of Fox and News Corp, Streisand moved to another family power struggle: Jeanie Buss versus her five siblings over whether to sell the family’s remaining 17.8 percent Lakers stake. Her brothers and sister want to cash out alongside billionaire Mark Walter, who bought control of the team in 2025 and has agreed to sell to Bob Iger and Joshua Kushner at a $12.5 billion valuation. Buss wants to retain the shares. A hearing is set for Dec. 8.

Wasser doesn’t just handle divorces; she models for them, fronting fashion label Reformation’s “Divorce Collection” campaign this year. For Sia, she filed an October settlement keeping the singer the primary custodial parent of her toddler with Dan Bernad, who receives $42,500 a month in child support. Her newest client is Hila Klein, who filed to divorce husband and H3 Podcast co-host Ethan Klein on Nov. 2 after publicly accusing him of an affair.

Wigdor is leaving no stone unturned in Julia Ormond’s ongoing sexual assault suit against Harvey Weinstein — pursuing Disney, Miramax and CAA for allegedly failing to protect her. His firm even won an order requiring former CAA chairman Michael Ovitz to answer questions under oath about the agency’s representation of the actress. Other clients include Cassie Ventura, whose rape and sex trafficking suit against Sean “Diddy” Combs settled a day after filing. The firm is also suing Leon Black, alleging the financier filed retaliatory lawsuits to intimidate lawyers representing his sexual assault accusers.

For Zwicker’s wealthy clients, moving away can be as complicated as passing a fortune down. Alongside estate planning for entertainers and multinational families, she advises on the tax consequences of changing countries. California, for one, doesn’t surrender its taxpayers easily. She told The San Francisco Standard that auditors examine ATM withdrawals and Apple Pay transactions to determine whether residents have really left — or just their tax bills. “They want to see all of the data on all of your devices,” she says.

How to deploy an app you built with AI to your own server

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at opscrew.dev

Originally published on the OpsCrew blog.

To deploy an AI-built app to your own server, you do five things: get the code into a Git repository, find out whether it is static files or a server, decide where its backend lives, build it into a container image with the right environment variables, and run that image behind a reverse proxy that serves your domain over HTTPS. Lovable, Bolt.new, Replit, v0 and Claude Code all take this path; what differs is the stack each one produces and the backend it leans on.

Every builder fact below comes from the builder's own documentation, checked on 11 November 2026. The Dockerfiles and the Caddy and Docker Compose files in the builder guides were built and run in Docker the same day, on Node.js 22, against fresh apps from the Vite, TanStack Start and Next.js starters and a small Express and PostgreSQL app.

Every builder hosts what it builds: Lovable publishes to lovable.app with custom domains and a managed backend, Bolt has Bolt Cloud hosting with .bolt.host addresses, Replit publishes to its own Google Cloud infrastructure, and v0 publishes to Vercel. Lovable's own docs recommend starting on Lovable, syncing the code with Git, and moving parts only when you hit a real constraint. Stay where you are when:

Move when you need something the builder's hosting does not give you: a fixed monthly price, data in a region or on hardware you choose, other services on the same machine, root access, or an app that no longer depends on one vendor. The comparisons of your own server against Vercel and against hosting platforms in general go through the trade-offs.

How each builder gets its code to GitHub, checked 11 November 2026

Then clone the repository and run the app on your own computer. If it does not start locally, it will not start on a server, and a local run shows you every environment variable it expects.

Open package.json and look at the dependencies and the build and start scripts.

The stacks AI builders produce and what each needs to run

Most builder apps keep their data and sign-in in a hosted service that the code reaches over the internet and that does not move with it:

Move the frontend first and keep the backend where it is. Once the app runs on your server against it, decide whether to move the data too, as a separate step with its own test.

Build-time public variables. Vite exposes variables that start with VITE_ to the browser and replaces them in the code at build time; Next.js does the same with NEXT_PUBLIC_. Their values end up inside the JavaScript every visitor downloads, so:

Runtime variables. Everything else, read on the server while the app runs: DATABASE_URL, API secrets, session secrets. These go into the container's environment on the server, from a file that never enters the repository. A server-rendered app can need a public value twice: a Lovable TanStack Start app reads its Supabase URL and key as VITE_ values in the browser and again as SUPABASE_URL and SUPABASE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY on the server.

Collect both lists before you build. Lovable keeps its public Supabase values in the repository's .env; Bolt keeps environment variables in .env but its secrets only on Bolt's servers; Replit's secrets must be re-entered; v0 keeps everything in the Vercel project's settings.

The Dockerfile depends on the stack from step 2; each builder article has one that was built and run for that stack:

Every stack gets the same front door: a reverse proxy takes the public traffic on ports 80 and 443 and passes requests to the app. Caddy also handles the domain's HTTPS certificate by itself: when the domain's DNS record points at the server and ports 80 and 443 are open, it obtains and renews it and redirects HTTP to HTTPS. This Caddyfile is the whole configuration for an app that listens on port 3000:

This compose.yaml runs the app and Caddy together, with the certificate kept in a volume so it survives restarts:

Replace app.example.com with your domain; on the server, docker compose up -d --build builds the image and starts both. Build-time variables from step 4 go under build.args, as each builder guide shows. Only Caddy's ports are published to the internet, never the app's. We ran these files with localhost as the site address, where Caddy issues a certificate from its own local authority; a public certificate for a real domain needs a real server and DNS record, which this test did not cover.

Any Linux server with Docker works, including one you already pay for. The general steps are the same for every app: how to deploy a web app to a VPS covers creating the server, SSH keys, the firewall and Docker; Docker Compose on Hetzner with GitHub Actions and a DigitalOcean Droplet walk through two providers; and Hetzner vs DigitalOcean helps you pick one.

For the domain, add an A record (and an AAAA record if the server has IPv6) pointing at the server's address. Caddy can only get the certificate after that record resolves, so set it before the first start.

Once the app is live, add deploys without downtime, so an update does not drop visitors, and a preview for every pull request, which replaces the builder's preview link.

Check each of these on a temporary address while the builder's hosting keeps running. Once all pass, move the domain and unpublish the old copy when you are ready.

What breaks after you move an AI-built app, and why

Lovable apps, and Bolt apps whose database you claimed into Supabase, sign users in through Supabase. Its Site URL is the default redirect address that confirmation and password-reset emails use, and its Redirect URLs list says where users may be sent after sign-in. In a Supabase project of your own, both are on the dashboard's URL Configuration page; for Lovable Cloud, Lovable's hosting guide says only to add the new address to the auth provider's allowed redirect URLs.

OpsCrew is an open-source tool that takes an app from its GitHub repository to a hardened server on your own cloud account. It recognises code exported from Lovable, Bolt, Replit and v0, and its free opscrew scan checks the repository for leaked keys and exposed Supabase or Firebase data before you go live.

It builds the container image in GitHub Actions with its own Dockerfile for Vite and Next.js apps and Node.js servers such as Express (or yours, if the repository has one), and handles VITE_ and NEXT_PUBLIC_ values as build-time values that are public by nature. It deploys to a new server on Hetzner Cloud or DigitalOcean, or to a server you already have, buys or connects your domain on Cloudflare, and runs a database next to your app when you want one, or keeps the hosted one you already use. Every change waits for your approval of its exact plan. In Claude Code, the OpsCrew plugin runs the same flow from the chat. The setup page lists the accounts and tools you need, and use cases shows what it does for an app from an AI builder.

SpaceX Just Raised $32 Billion for AI Chips. Lenders Want Elon Musk to Keep Them on Earth.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SpaceX had to pay a yield above 8% on the biggest piece of a $32 billion bond sale to buy Nvidia chips.

By Johnny Rice – Nov 20, 2026 at 12:28PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

Space Exploration Technologies Corp. (SPCX -7.02%) sold $32 billion of bonds through a trust set up to buy Nvidia (NVDA -6.81%) chips on Thursday, with the largest piece paying investors a yield above 8%.

And, according to reporting from The Financial Times, the lenders involved wanted guarantees that SpaceX would keep the financed chips on terra firma, apparently worried about the logistics of repossessing chips circling the Earth at about 17,500 mph.

The stock was down 6.7% as of 11:39 a.m. ET on Friday as the whole market sees red.

The bonds are part of a more than $40 billion financing package SpaceX announced in October. To try to keep the debt off its books, the financing runs through a separate entity -- a trust that sells the bonds, buys the chips, and leases them to SpaceX. Similar maneuvers are increasingly common across the AI data center market as companies look for ways to deal with ballooning debt balances.

Functionally, however, SpaceX is still the one borrowing, and its quarterly lease payments are what pay the bondholders back.

The largest piece, about $25.2 billion, was priced at a yield of 8.259%. The average yield on high-grade U.S. corporate debt was 5.95% that day, so investors are being paid about 2.3 percentage points more than usual for bonds that are supposed to be relatively safe. Bonds considered "junk" were yielding an average of 8.3% that day.

The loans are secured by the chips in the same way your mortgage is secured by your home. If SpaceX ever defaulted, the lenders' fallback would be to repossess the GPUs and sell them. That's obviously quite difficult if they're in space. It's not clear if the lenders were able to get anything binding in the final documents, but SpaceX says these chips are for its "terrestrial compute build-out."

The chips, destined for SpaceX data centers, are expected to start arriving in January. They're part of the massive build-out SpaceX's AI division is in the thick of, which has driven enormous growth on its top-line. The AI unit nearly tripled its sales between Q2 and Q3, growing revenue from $2.6 billion to $7.6 billion. Costs are high, however: the business spent $16.4 billion in capex in its Q3 -- the lion's share of the company's overall $19.6 billion capex spend.

While the terms are less favorable than past deals, SpaceX got the money it was seeking. CFO Bret Johnsen said in the company's October financing announcement that SpaceX is seeing "less than one year payback periods" on its infrastructure investments.

But I'm still skeptical long-term. Debt market investors are incredibly sophisticated, and watching spreads -- the gap between what a U.S. Treasury pays and what the bond yields -- widen for SpaceX doesn't do much to convince me that the economics make sense. Right now, the payoff looks incredible. I don't think that's going to hold up.

Electric Vans — Are They The Final EV Frontier? Kia PV5.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We took a trip to see the Kia PV5 electric van. Our brief takeaway: “Kool like a Kia.” Should keep the bean counters happy.

Inspired by a comment in the Electric Vehicle Council report about electric vans, Majella and I decided to have a short visit to a group of dealerships under the Norris Motors banner. We hoped to check out the LDV eDeliver 5 van, touted as selling for under AU$50,000. However, there was no stock to view and we ended up chatting to the dealer and then moving on to the MG U9 BEV ute. You can read about it here. And then we found the Kia PV5 delivery van.

Julie Delvecchio, the CEO of EVC, calls the lack of affordable electric vans in Australia “the single biggest unclaimed opportunity left.” We think that is about to change. Since its launch in May, 496 PV5 have sold in Australia.

The Kia PV5 reminded me a lot of the Renault Kangoo in both size and shape, and it is able to carry about the same payload. And the front looked like the BMW i3. The one we looked at had been sold and was awaiting a fitout as a camper van. The Norris Group Kia specialist was generous with his time and the information he shared. He has been selling the Kia EV5 for the past 5 months and delivered 5 of the vans. He sold his first one within a week of its arrival on the lot — to Ray, a driver working for Amazon.

Ray appreciated the 390 km range. My friendly sales rep tells me that this will be increased in 2027 models to 420 km. Ray was getting out of diesel and most of his questions were around charging and regen braking. Most of his trips were around the burbs and he was not intending to take the van on the highway. The Kia PV5 is a workhorse, with a passenger car feel and level of comfort. It even has electric lumbar support for the driver — great when you are spending so much time in the van delivering parcels in the lead-up to Christmas. It certainly has a lot more tech than the diesel he was trading in.

In fact, he was “blown away” by the standard features — the 360-degree camera, for example. “Vans are not known for their visibility.” On his return to the dealership for his first service, he commented on the smoothness of the torque: “It has no issues going uphill.” He reported that the van felt “well planted on the road.” Ray has gotten used to trickle charging at home and has lost his range anxiety. Like most new EV drivers, once he started living and working with the vehicle, his fears vanished. The PV5 supports fast charging also for when Ray needs a boost on the road.

But what about that all-important question of cost? Can we make the bean counters happy? The Kia PV5 retails for AU$56,000 (before on-road costs), which is about the same price as comparable diesel vans like the Ford Transit and the Toyota HiAce. Add to that the savings on fuel, the peace of mind from fuel security, and the reduced servicing and it is easy to build a business case for the Kia PV5 — cost per parcel delivery. The PV5 even comes with vehicle to load.

And it isn’t made in China! Can the PV5 replace Toyota’s HiAce? Find the answer in this video. The reviewer points out many features that make the PV5 an attractive option: the charge port is on the front. The van has lots of nooks and crannies for storage. She measures storage by capacity to fit in bottles of wine — for example, two in the glove box. There is a step provided to alight the van, aided by grab handles. More storage space is provided above the steering wheel, including a nifty phone slot. There are customizable buttons on the steering wheel to create shortcuts; for example, to the air conditioning settings. A wireless phone charger is nestled near the dash. The glovebox contains the V2L adaptor supplied with the van.

The fact that the van reverts to the lowest regen setting every time you restart the car is a little niggle. That, and there is no frunk — reducing the reviewer’s all-important need for wine storage. Full specs can be found here. Kia even gives you helpful advice on how to apply for electric vehicle subsidies (last page, bottom right). One of the pictures that caught my eye shows the PV5 as a flatbed truck.

The PV5 was recently named 2026 International Van of the Year. It measures 4695mm long, 2255mm wide, and 1899mm tall on a 2995mm wheelbase. Range is stated as 416 km, but will of course vary with driving style and load. The battery is a 71.2kWh nickel cobalt manganese (NCM) lithium-ion battery. Kia offers a 165,000 km warranty on the battery. The van is front-wheel drive.

The PV5 has over 4 cubic metres of cargo capacity, double both that of the ID Buzz and the Tesla Model Y. Space enough for two euro pallets. There are multiple access points, with sliding doors on both sides and barn doors in the rear. The rear barn doors open to 180 degrees, and a low 419mm load height improves accessibility.

Goods being shipped are protected by padding on the rear doors and insides of the back. The PV5 can carry up to 740 kg. Tie downs on the floor of the rear of the van help to secure cargo. There is also a power point in back of van. Perhaps a tradie can recharge their tools on site. No frunk for wine.

From the Spec sheet: “Standard driver assist features include blind-spot collision avoidance, navigation-based adaptive cruise control, lane-keep assist, lane centring with hands-on detection, and parking collision avoidance assist.”

A launch in the USA is financially difficult because of the 25% “chicken tax” .  Kia builds the PV5 in South Korea and classifies it as a commercial vehicle, it faces a 25% US import tariff on light trucks and commercial vans.

Sadly, the Kia PV5 doesn’t come in red, but only in white, grey and black. Most companies may take up the option of wrapping the van in their own livery. Overall, we were impressed with Kia PV5 and looking forward to seeing more of them on the road throughout the suburbs as Australian delivery drivers move towards a bright electric future.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

The Privacy Policy URL Nobody Was Watching

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at meridianbuild.dev

The URL pointed to a GitHub Pages site for the app's repository. A few weeks earlier I had restricted what that Pages site served, for good reasons, and later it stopped serving the policy at all. Nothing in my setup knew that a store console depended on that one path. The site changed, the link broke, and the only system that noticed was Google's review pipeline, weeks later.

The App Store listing had a different URL: a GitHub gist. It worked, it loaded, Apple had accepted it. The quick fix was obvious: paste the same gist URL into Play Console, both stores aligned, done. I did that.

It dated from March, from an earlier version of the app. It mentioned collecting an email address and using PostHog for analytics. The current app does neither. It uses anonymous accounts with an animal pseudonym, and RevenueCat for the one in-app purchase. The real, current policy existed. It was in the repository as an HTML page, written in French and English in October, covering both Apple and Google. It just was not at any URL either store knew about.

So for months the App Store had pointed parents at a policy describing data collection the app no longer did, and for some time Google Play had pointed them at nothing. And for a few hours on November 20, after my "fix", both stores pointed at the outdated one.

I wanted a URL I control, on a domain that is not tied to how one repository happens to be published. This blog already runs as a static site with automatic deploys, so the policy now lives here:

https://meridianbuild.dev/kidcapital/privacy/

It is a plain copy of the HTML page from the app repository, dropped into the blog's public/kidcapital/privacy/ folder, so the static build serves it as is. No framework, no layout, nothing that a future blog redesign can break by accident.

The cost of this setup is that the policy now exists in two places, the app repository and the blog. Any future change has to be made in both. I wrote that down where I will see it, because "two copies" is exactly how the gist went stale.

Fixing the URL surfaced the next layer. Apple's privacy label for the app, the "Data collected" section on the App Store page, still declares the categories from March: contact info, diagnostics, usage data. Those match the old gist, not the app. Play's Data safety form and the current policy say something different. That is the next thing to realign, and it matters more than the URL, because the label is what most parents actually look at.

I am mentioning it because it is the honest state of things. Fixing one field in a store console tends to reveal the three next to it that were filled in the same week, by the same tired person, for a version of the app that no longer exists.

If you ship an app, open your store consoles today, click every URL you declared, and read what is on the other end. It does not take long. I had not done it since the day I filled those fields.

If you are setting up release automation for a small app, I wrote about using one version number for iOS and Android in CI, which is where this list of console chores started.

Write SHA-256 from scratch in under 60 lines of Python

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SHA-256 is everywhere: git object ids, TLS certificates, password storage schemes, package lockfiles, blockchains. Most of us call hashlib.sha256() and move on. But the inside is surprisingly small: some bit rotations, some additions modulo 2^32, and a loop that runs 64 times.

In this post we will write SHA-256 from scratch in Python, in under 60 lines, and check it against hashlib on thousands of random inputs.

Everything works on 32 bit unsigned integers, so we need a mask and a right rotation:

Python integers are unbounded, so we mask after every addition to emulate 32 bit overflow.

The rule: append a single 1 bit, then 0 bits until the length is 56 bytes mod 64, then the original length in bits as a 64 bit big endian integer.

0x80 is 10000000 in binary: the 1 bit followed by seven zeros. A classic bug is writing the length in bytes instead of bits, or little endian instead of big endian. Both produce a hash that looks perfectly plausible and is completely wrong.

Note that a 55 byte message fits in one block, but a 56 byte message needs two, because the length field no longer fits. Test that boundary.

SHA-256 uses two tables of constants. They look random but they are "nothing up my sleeve" numbers:

You can regenerate them with integer math to convince yourself:

Shifting left by 96 before the cube root (or 64 before the square root) gives us 32 fractional bits, and the mask throws away the integer part. In the full listing below the tables are written out as literals, which is how most implementations ship them.

Each 64 byte block becomes sixteen 32 bit big endian words. SHA-256 then expands them to 64 words, mixing earlier words with rotations and shifts:

Watch the last term in s0 and s1: it is a plain shift (>>), not a rotation. Mixing those up is the most common mistake in hand written SHA-256.

The state is eight words, traditionally called a to h. Each round computes two temporary values and shifts the words down by one position:

Two small functions do the nonlinear work:

~e in Python is negative, but the & g with a 32 bit value brings it back into range, so no extra mask is needed there.

After the 64 rounds, add the result back into the previous state word by word. That feed forward is what makes the compression function hard to invert.

Then compare against hashlib on random inputs, making sure to cover every length around the padding boundary:

This passes for every length from 0 to 199 bytes plus a few large inputs. It is slow compared to hashlib (pure Python, one bit operation at a time), and it is for learning only: use your standard library for anything real.

Once you have SHA-256, the natural next step is HMAC: H((K xor opad) || H((K xor ipad) || message)). It is only a few more lines and it lets you sign and verify tokens. When you verify, compare the signatures in constant time (hmac.compare_digest in Python) so you do not leak timing information.

If you want to build this step by step with tests at every stage (padding, message schedule, one round, full digest, HMAC and signed tokens) in any language, we put together a guided project for it: Build SHA-256 and HMAC from scratch. It is in beta, so feedback is very welcome.

Shrinking My Photos Did Not Cut My Gemini Bill

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at meridianbuild.dev

Every time someone adds a piece of clothing to their wardrobe in OutfitMaker, a background job sends the photo to Gemini on Vertex AI. The model returns a small JSON object: category, colour, a description, a few tags, translated labels, and which way the garment is rotated in the photo. That is the whole job. It runs on every upload, so it runs a lot.

On November 20 I read the Google Cloud bill line by line. The top line was image input for that analysis call, about 15 euros over 30 days. Not a disaster, but it was the biggest one, and it grows with every new user who photographs their closet.

Phones take large photos. My first idea was the one everybody has: resize the image to something modest before encoding it, send fewer bytes, pay less.

Before writing that code I checked how Gemini counts image tokens. It does not bill by bytes or by pixels in the way I assumed. An image becomes a fixed number of tokens determined by the resolution setting of the request, and the model scales whatever you send to fit that budget. A huge photo and a small photo of the same shirt land on the same token count.

So resizing on my side would have cost CPU on the worker, added a dependency on image processing in a code path that already had its own share of trouble with missing binaries, and changed nothing on the invoice. Smaller uploads are still nice for storage and bandwidth. They are just not a Gemini cost lever.

The lever is mediaResolution in generationConfig. It tells the model how much detail to keep from each image, and the token count follows. The values are MEDIA_RESOLUTION_LOW, MEDIA_RESOLUTION_MEDIUM and MEDIA_RESOLUTION_HIGH, and if you do not set it, you get the default.

Lowering detail on an image classifier sounds risky, so I did not pick a value from the docs. I ran the real call in production on 12 garment photos at each setting: default, MEDIUM, LOW. For each photo I compared category, colour and rotation, and read promptTokenCount from the response metadata.

The prompt token count includes the text of my prompt, which does not change between runs. So the drop on the image part alone is larger than the 40% you see in the table.

LOW was tempting. It is cheaper again, and on 11 of the 12 photos it was right. But colour is the field users notice first. If the app calls a navy sweater black, every outfit built on it inherits the mistake, and the user's trust in the whole wardrobe drops with it. One error in twelve is too many for the field people check with their own eyes. MEDIUM gave identical answers, so MEDIUM it is.

It is three lines in the service plus a constant, shipped as PR #291 (commit 356dbbc in a private repo):

I left the test results in a comment above the field, with the numbers. The next person tempted to switch to LOW to save a bit more, which will probably be me, should see why I did not.

And one line in an existing test that builds the request body:

That assertion looks trivial. It is there because this kind of setting disappears silently. Someone refactors the request builder, the field is dropped, every test still passes because the model answers fine at default resolution, and the bill goes back up without a single error. A test that pins a cost setting is the only alarm you get for that kind of regression.

My estimate is a saving of around 6 euros a month at the current volume. That is small in absolute terms. It is also free, permanent, and it scales with uploads, which is the one usage pattern I hope keeps growing.

The more useful outcome for me is the habit. Before optimising an AI call, I now check how the provider actually counts the units I pay for. My intuition said bytes. The answer was a setting I had never read, sitting one level below maxOutputTokens in the same config hash.

If you send images to Gemini, look at your generationConfig. If mediaResolution is not there, you are paying for the default on every call. Run your own handful of real inputs at MEDIUM and LOW, compare the fields your users actually look at, and pick the lowest setting that gives the same answers.

How the surge in AI-discovered bugs is straining Microsoft security and threatening tech stability

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Angela Gunn on Nov 20, 2026 at 9:25 amNovember 20, 2026 at 9:25 am

It has come to the attention of the general public this month that adversaries successfully adopt technology a lot faster than defenders do. This has nothing to do with the willingness of defenders to try. It has everything to do with the ancient truth that to succeed, defenders have to get it right every time but adversaries only have to get it right occasionally.

Now multiply “every time” and “occasionally” by AI. Do you like that math? Does anyone?

The disastrous update that broke Teams, Outlook, SharePoint and other packages was not a direct attack by AI-wielding bad guys. Nor was the FAA outage. Nor were most of the outage events of the past few weeks, though who can say what’s up with those European Union refrigerators.

No. The true AI-driven mayhem has been predicated for several months now not on powerful new AI-honed attacks, but on an absolute deluge of bugs of relatively low severity that Microsoft and other defenders with a formal patch process aren’t built to process.

The question is whether traditional patch programs can adapt their processes to handle the deluge. Microsoft’s is MAPP (Microsoft Active Protections Program), to which the company has been committed for nearly two decades. If the industry-spanning events of the past few weeks are any indication, these programs can’t.

Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella said Wednesday at the New York Times DealBook Summit that last week’s Microsoft 365 outage came from a latent bug and wasn’t directly tied to AI, but he warned that AI-driven patching could bring more instability.

He elaborated in a separate appearance on CNBC the same day, saying the flood of patches being applied to older infrastructure is making systems less stable.

AI has “allowed us to find these bugs and fix these bugs, but that means you’ve got to then patch them, and that creates instability,” Nadella said, when asked by Jim Cramer about a string of recent outages at United, Schwab, Chase and other organizations.

Some context: MAPP turned 18 this year. The program, run by the MSRC (Microsoft Security Response Center), handles outreach to partner companies that gain early access to information on the bugs in advance of each month’s Patch Tuesday release. In-house, it coordinates the patch release process, just as the larger MSRC organization helps coordinate the handling of bug reports from external finders.

MSRC also runs point on prioritizing patches of the worst bugs through its SSIRP (Software and Services Incident Response Plan) program, itself dating to 2004. In its time, it was an industry leader in how companies can efficiently get their arms around bad bugs.

MAPP in its own way is similarly remarkable. It has undergone a few traumas over the years, usually when a partner breaks confidence and leaks the advance details it receives. In 2025, after attacks on SharePoint raised suspicions of a leak from the program, Microsoft stopped giving Chinese companies early access. But MAPP continues to tick along in a way that would make any program manager proud. Other companies have their own version of MAPP / MSRC, as do community efforts such as those supporting Linux.

Full disclosure: I’m a former MSRC employee who learned about MAPP at the knee of the program’s principals. I subsequently tracked patch releases at a company partnered with MAPP at its highest (Validate) level, which means I was privy to the 14-day multi-stage run-up to each month’s release. I admire and respect the program… and I’m afraid that in the AI-finder era, it’s in big, big trouble.

Since January 2021, Microsoft has issued patches for an estimated 10,319 CVEs. (I use estimated numbers throughout this analysis because there are various ways of counting such things, including whether or not the patch accrues to an application such as Edge, which actually relies on the Chromium project for its fixes. Other analysts arrive at larger or smaller counts.)

Over half of those — 5,383 CVEs — are 2026 releases, 5,128 of which were addressed since April. In comparison, 2025’s full-year CVE total was 1,180.

What happened in April? As nearly as analysis can determine, the floodgates opened and finders (security researchers) started barraging the company with reports.

The first quarter of 2026 saw 255 CVEs addressed over three months. April had 163, May 132; in June the number jumped to 209. In July it was off to the races with 575 CVEs. In October, the monthly count topped 1,400.

Most of these bugs aren’t coming from the usual security stalwarts. They’re coming from new finders, they’re coming in with multiple simultaneous finders not believed to be working together, and they’re coming in relatively weak.

Though the overall volume has skyrocketed, the number per month of bugs under active exploit in the wild — the notorious “zero-days” — hasn’t increased proportionally.

Moreover, average bug severity has in fact decreased. Until 2026, the average CVSS severity score for all bugs tended to hover around 7.8 (out of 10) each month. In 2026, that’s dropped to approximately 7.4 as of September — not garbage finds, but the sort of thing that doesn’t scramble the jets.

No surge in zero-days and a drop in severity — that’s good news. The problem is the volume.

Once bugs are received by Microsoft, each goes through a MAPP-helmed, multi-team process of investigation and patch development.

The true AI-driven mayhem has been predicated not on powerful new AI-honed attacks, but on a deluge of bugs of relatively low severity that Microsoft and others aren’t built to process.

Since many products have several versions still in support, that often means that multiple teams within each product group are asked to ingest a bug report, replicate the problem, devise and test a fix, ensure the fix doesn’t trigger other problems or inadvertently point to a similar bug elsewhere in the product and ready it for mass consumption. Even AI slop has to be taken seriously, just in case.

Meanwhile, MAPP is the traffic cop for the entire release, working with each team to ascertain patches are ready, understand the nature of the bugs and what can be safely communicated to partners and the public, and ready the processes that make the second Tuesday of each month a festival of downloads and reboots. From where we stand right now, it’s all fairly… hand-crafted.

In theory the process is automatable to some extent, though the success of that effort so far in light of recent events is debatable. But high volume also leads to other problems, such as exposing information on incomplete patching.

For instance, Microsoft has recently patched Office bugs that apply to both Windows and Mac, but patches have initially only been available for Windows versions.

Microsoft’s release-day information about such bugs — patching Windows versions while asking Apple users to monitor Microsoft communications for news of their own patch — alerts the world, including eager adversaries, that the flaw exists. Apple customers just have to hope no adversaries exploit it while their patch is pending.

More broadly, many of the steps in the current patching process involve judgment calls, not processes that scale well or that can reliably be handled by existing AI-driven systems. In Microsoft’s case, MAPP is a remarkably small team of humans and, being security folk at Microsoft, they’re not always the loudest or most popular voice in Redmond.

Add the mercurial nature of finders, who may or may not provide full details on their bugs (ask any MSRC person how often they receive a crash dump and a demand for payment) and may or may not disclose their finds publicly if they don’t feel they’re getting sufficient response.

Do not add the kind of defender staffing needed to handle a massive increase in volume — either in MAPP or in the individual product groups building the actual patches — because in the AI era, it’s likely the companies won’t add it either.

The cracks are showing. Microsoft has been compelled to issue OOB (out-of-band) releases and servicing updates monthly since July, often within just a few days of the original release.

OOBs are important enough to merit release outside the usual patch cadence, often because there’s a security issue involved, but they’re tough on administrators already wading through a slew of regularly scheduled patching. With volumes high, patch testers may rush or elide certain steps, such as checking that a fix doesn’t break something deeper in the system.

Nadella told the DealBook audience this week that a patch for a critical bank system might once have been tested for a week. Now, he said, the patch “needs to go instantly from being available to being patched in production because it’s being exploited in the wild. So that’s going to create instability.”

Think of MAPP as Mariners catcher Dan Wilson in 2001: a pro handling a staff of skilled human pitchers, one pitch at a time. Now it’s 2026, and the pitchers have been swapped for a bank of mediocre pitching machines all slinging fastballs at … 2026-era Dan Wilson.

Here, again, AI can’t fix a system that requires defenders to catch every pitch. Secondary lines of defense that have worked in the past — for instance, the requirement by various regulatory programs that enterprises ascertain that patches are safe for their own systems — rely heavily on companies sending out the soundest possible patches.

Managed service providers might at least make sure that patches are flagged to customers as available, relevant and urgent. But they’re in no position to do much more than trust that what’s shipped is safe to deploy and react when things go wrong.

Even that gets harder when a bug kneecaps the security tools they depend on. Last week’s Microsoft 365 outage affected parts of Microsoft Defender that interact with Exchange Online.

It’s not just Microsoft, of course. The Linux community is seriously suffering, and both Apple and (especially) Google have struggled with patch-release quality in 2026 as evidenced by their own emergency updates and post-release device instability reports. Industry-wide, ecosystems built around reliable, transparent patch cadences and patch-development processes have never seemed more in danger of being overwhelmed.

What needs to happen? Sure, build those AI systems; the marketing people will be pleased. It’ll speed up some parts of the process. But a system capable of replicating, every time, the human judgment calls that go into the patch-release process isn’t going to magically create itself; people have to create it.

Is there some chance that the deluge will ebb — that researchers and their tools will at some point finally find all the bugs in all the world’s products?

It’s a pretty thought, and some relief may eventually come. When more and more unrelated finders turn up the same bug, it gives one hope that the set is finite. But no one knows when relief will come, and most folk in tech suspect that really, no, there is no bottom to the bug barrel. Nor, for the foreseeable future, any rest for the weary.

Angela Gunn is a GRC consultant and vCISO with Ericius Security. She has worked in infosec and incident response roles for companies including Microsoft, HP and BAE Systems Applied Intelligence. Prior to working in the tech sector, she covered it as a journalist for publications including USA Today, PC Magazine, Computerworld, Yahoo! Internet Life and Seattle Weekly.

Subagents, Built Live: Reviewer, Tester, Researcher, Doc Writer

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at youtube.com

The video version of this article, "Subagents, Built Live: Reviewer, Tester, Researcher, Doc Writer," goes public on YouTube on Friday, November 20, 2026, and shows all four configs written live in a real repo. This article is the written version, laid out so you can copy the structure.

Most people create a subagent by writing a personality. Friendly. Helpful. Expert at everything. Then they give it every tool in the box and wonder why it drifts, edits files it should only have read, and reports success nobody can check. That is a coworker with no job description and a master key.

A subagent is not a second generalist. It is a narrow brief with permissions attached: one job, a defined set of files it may read or write, a fixed report shape, and a list of things it must never do. The personality is irrelevant. The boundaries are the product.

This article builds four of them on the demo project from the earlier videos: a reviewer, a test writer, a researcher, and a doc writer.

Permissions are what separate roles, not tone of voice. If two subagents can both edit everything, you do not have roles. You have copies.

Two rules sit underneath all four configs. The reviewer is read only. And the one writer rule: exactly one agent has write access to a given file at a time, so subagents that need the same file run in sequence, never in parallel.

The reviewer's job, in one sentence: check a finished diff against the original packet and the repo rules, and report what is wrong.

Its packet has four fields: the original packet, the diff, the files changed, and the test command, from the repo rules file, not invented on the spot.

Its permissions are the whole design. Read any file. Run the test suite and read the output. Write nothing. No edits, no formatting fixes, no helpful little commits. Its report: findings first, by severity, each naming the file, what is wrong, and the evidence. Then what it checked, what it did not check, and a verdict: accept, or send back. Never do: never fix a finding yourself, never soften one to be polite, never approve work you did not read, never edit tests.

The test writer's job: write tests for a stated behavior, from the spec in its packet. Tests only. Never the implementation.

Its packet: the behavior spec with examples, the function signature, and the existing test file to copy style from. Permissions: write only in tests, only the file named in the packet. Read the app code, run the suite, touch no implementation.

In the demo, its new tests fail, because the function under test is still a stub. That failure is the correct result, and its report says so plainly: tests written, command run, real result, cases left out. Never do: never edit a test to match wrong output, never write the implementation to make its own tests pass, never delete or weaken an existing test, never report a skipped test as passing. Tests are the spec.

The researcher's job: answer one specific question from named sources, and return a short brief the orchestrator can act on.

Its packet is one question in one sentence, the decision that answer will feed, and the sources it may use, official docs first. If the question takes two sentences, it is two packets. Permissions: read only on the repo, plus the named sources. It writes nothing in the repo. Its only product is the brief.

The brief has a fixed shape: the answer in the first two lines, then evidence with each source named, then what it could not confirm, labelled unconfirmed and never guessed at. Never do: never turn a brief into a code change, never present an unconfirmed claim as a fact, never widen the question, never bury the answer. Bulk reading is a cheaper model job, safe only because the format does the disciplining.

The doc writer's job: turn finished, verified work into documentation that matches what the code actually does: a README section, a usage example, a changelog entry.

It runs last, and its packet proves it: the accepted diff, the reviewer verdict, and a usage example that has actually been run. No accepted diff, no docs. Permissions: write only in the documentation files named in its packet, read code and tests freely, run examples, change no code, ever. If code and intended docs disagree, the disagreement goes in its report, not papered over. Its report is the doc change, the example with its real run output, the disagreements list, and what it left out.

Never do: never document a feature that is not in the accepted code, never invent example output, never describe planned behavior in the present tense. The example run is not optional.

Now the four on one task, in order, with who writes what.

The test writer goes first, writes only the test file, and its tests fail honestly against the stub. Then one worker implements the change in one file. The reviewer goes next and writes nothing: it reads the diff, runs the full suite, and returns findings or a verdict. If a factual question came up, the researcher answers it in a brief; it never enters the code path itself. The doc writer goes last, after acceptance, with an example it ran itself.

At every step you can point at exactly who was allowed to write the file that changed. Fail that pointing test and you do not have subagents. You have a crowd.

The pattern to design against is a reviewer with write access. It finds a real problem in a diff. Instead of reporting it, it quietly fixes the code itself, because fixing feels more helpful than reporting.

Three things are lost at once. Nobody learns the worker made the mistake. The fix was never reviewed, because the reviewer checked its own work. And the report says clean, so the record shows a diff that never had a problem. A lie by omission.

You do not fix this with a sterner prompt. You fix it structurally, by taking away the write permission. Read only is the first permission decision in every config here.

The project templates and orchestration checklist from this series are free in the Multi Agent Starter Pack. The four full subagent configs built in the video are the core of the Multi Agent Coding Ops Kit that launches in December:

The video version of this article, "Subagents, Built Live: Reviewer, Tester, Researcher, Doc Writer," goes public Friday, November 20, 2026 on the Second Shift Dev YouTube channel, to watch the four configs built and run live.

The day job ends. The agent team clocks in.

Choosing whether to sell to Donnie or Brian isn't just about who pays more

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After the Clean Up After Donnie GTA 6 mission, Donnie becomes a fence you can sell narcotics, marked notes, and jewelry to, all you have to do is drop them in the deposit box at his trailer in Key Lento. But with Brian also a fence, you may be wondering whether it's more beneficial to sell your goods to Donnie or Brian. While one may offer more lucrative rewards, all that glitters isn't necessarily gold in GTA 6.

If you hover your cursor over a fencing location icon on the GTA 6 map, you can see the rate each respective fence is offering for narcotics, marked bills, and jewelry. In GTA 6 Chapter 1, Donnie (Donnie's Trailer) pays low for narcotics, and very low for jewelry and marked bills.

Brian (Brian's Lockup), on the other hand, pays very low for all three types of illicit products. As such, Donnie pays more than Brian for narcotics. For example, if you collect all the cocaine drops Cal tells you about after the Clean Up After Donnie mission and fence them, Donnie offers you $3,400, while Brian offers just $2,720. Do note that the time it takes for these payments to reach your mailbox can be impacted by any available missions.

While Donnie pays more, Cal suggests you sell the cocaine drops you collect to Brian, by depositing them at Brian's Lockup in Key Lento. He doesn't say why, but he seems to know something you don't. You should listen to Cal and sell your goods to Brian as the events of a later mission, called Swamped, might impact any payments Donnie owes you in GTA 6 (be aware of major spoilers by following that link).

Note that if you sell to Brian and he seems to be taking his time to pay you, there may be an available Brian mission you must complete before your payment is sent to the mailbox at Jason's House.

Sprocket Security Named a Leader in GigaOm Radar for Penetration Testing as a Service

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One year after debuting as a Challenger and Outperformer, Sprocket earns perfect scores on four criteria as it pairs expert pentesters with a fleet of AI agents.

MADISON, Wis., Nov. 20, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Sprocket Security has been named a Leader in the GigaOm Radar for Penetration Testing as a Service (PTaaS) v5. Sprocket placed as a Leader in the report's Agile Optimizers scorecards and earned perfect marks on test launch efficiency, attack surface integration, rules of engagement controls, and the testing audit trail.

The placement marks a clear step up from 2025, when GigaOm recognized Sprocket as a Challenger and Outperformer. In the year between, Sprocket expanded its capabilities with a fleet of AI agents that work alongside its pentesters, extending how much of the attack surface gets tested and how quickly.

Sprocket Security has been named a Leader in the GigaOm Radar for Penetration Testing as a Service (PTaaS) v5.

This year's report, authored by GigaOm analyst Chris Ray, maps four buyer profiles to four quadrants. It frames five market themes around the questions buyers should ask before signing: who performs the test, how it reaches internal networks, and what evidence an auditor will accept?

"Sprocket Security earns its Leader position by pairing AI testing agents with human validation of every finding, and wrapping both in machine-enforced scope, a customer kill switch and a checksummed audit trail, so its testing keeps pace with a changing attack surface without giving up control," Ray shared.

Sprocket's top scores reflect how the platform runs day to day:

Test launch efficiency (5.0): Teams scope and start testing without weeks of kickoff calls and paperwork.

Attack surface integration (5.0): Testing follows the attack surface as it changes, so new assets get tested as they appear.

Rules of engagement controls (5.0): Customers set what gets tested, when, and how, with guardrails built into the platform.

Testing audit trail (5.0): Every action is logged, giving security teams and auditors a record of what was tested and what was found.

Sprocket was founded because point-in-time pentests couldn't keep up with how companies change. Continuous testing closed part of that gap. Sprocket's AI agents close more of it, running alongside its pentesters on the Sprocket platform:

Apex: an agentic pentester for unauthenticated web applications, mapping each app and testing its own theories about where it might break.

Link: an agentic pentester for authenticated web applications, testing what sits behind the login.

Pinion: an agentic pentester for internal networks, falling the attack paths worth chasing.

Sprocket's pentesters direct the work and validate what the agents find, so customers get more coverage without trading away the judgment of an experienced tester.

"A year ago GigaOm called us a Challenger. Today we are a Leader, and agentic innovation is why. We put a fleet of AI agents to work next to our pentesters for all types of testing, and the results show up in these scores," said Casey Cammilleri, CEO and founder of Sprocket Security. "Buyers are asking who is actually testing, whether it reaches the parts of the network attackers go after, and whether the results will hold up with an auditor. Our answer is experienced pentesters with agents that extend their reach, on a platform that shows its work."

Organizations that work with Sprocket Security get:

Sprocket will keep expanding its AI fleet and testing coverage. Every new agent is held to Sprocket's Safety Framework for Autonomous Offensive Security Agents, which requires agents to stay within scope, stop at proof, log every action, and let humans step in at any point. The aim is to give buyers clear answers to the questions GigaOm says to ask: who is testing, how far the testing reaches, and what evidence it leaves behind.

Security leaders can access a complimentary copy of the GigaOm Radar for Penetration Testing as a Service v5 at https://www.sprocketsecurity.com/gigaom.

Sprocket Security provides a technology-powered continuous penetration testing platform. Named in GigaOm and Gartner reports this year, Sprocket continuously monitors customer environments and triggers hybrid testing based on each customer's dynamic attack surface and emerging threats. Unlike legacy penetration testing, Sprocket's continuous approach delivers real-time insights and adaptive security, giving organizations a security posture that reflects today, not their last test.

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang accused of decades of bullying and workplace abuse in new investigation

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Public humiliation reportedly considered a "rite of passage".

An investigation into the workplace behavior of Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang, who resigned recently after a series of extramarital affairs came to light, has uncovered decades of harassment, verbal employee abuse and "cultish" behaviour at the company which has long played a major role in PC gaming.

Following Huang's departure, The New York Times has published a report in which "more than a dozen" current and former Nvidia employees discussed their experiences with the former CEO. Speaking on conditions of anonymity, employees alleged Jensen Huang would verbally berate them, reportedly going as far as to say he'd rip their arms off, rip their heart out and stomp on it, or rip their head off and "pee on your brain".

The report includes numerous examples of Huang's alleged abusive behaviour, which apparently stretches back years before Nvidia became the one of the most valuable companies in the world - years before the AI boom. It's claimed Huang on one occasion went around a room of managers telling them he'd kill them. Then, when he reached a person in their 70s, said everyone hoped they'd drop dead.

Huang allegedly referred to these bouts of public humiliation as a "rite of passage" at Nvidia, when questioned by an internal HR employee whom he later fired for not being a "right fit". Huan's stance was apparently to test whether a person could handle the culture there, which is described in the report as "cultish".

It's claimed Huang's influence at Nvidia has meant other senior managers have followed his lead, including his daughter Madison, Nvidia's chief of staff, who it's said often yells at staff, including executives more highly ranked than her.

A spokesperson for Huang denied the allegations in response to a request for comment by the New York Times. The spokesperson, John Fisher, said the claims in the report were "fabrications that are now impossible to disprove specifically, and the thousands of Nvidia employees who continue to speak out for Jensen show they’re false".

Huang resigned from his role as Nvidia CEO earlier this month after a series of extramarital affairs were unearthed - one with a person US intelligence believes to be a Chinese spy, and two others with Nvidia employees. The chair of Nvidia's board, Harvey C. Jones, reportedly said earlier this week that the company "does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO".

With a 4K panel and a 165Hz refresh rate, you're going to be very future-proofed if you also want a next-gen console at your desk at some point in the future. If you also play on PC, this is a solid 4K monitor option for you.

Nintendo reinstates SmileBasic 4 to the eShop after it was used for Switch 2 jailbreaks

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Get sneak previews, exclusive competitions and details of special events each month!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Finally, law-abiding Switch 2 users can rest easy

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

SmileBasic 4, one of the key pieces of software being used to jailbreak Switch 2 consoles, is back on the Nintendo eShop. While the console's firmware has long since been updated to stop known jailbreak exploits, SmileBasic 4 itself has now also been patched.

SmileBasic is a line of programming tools which developer SmileBoom has released for various Nintendo platforms, allowing users to code their own simple programs and games. It's also opened multiple backdoors for jailbreak exploits over the years, first on 3DS, and then on Switch 2.

Technically, SmileBasic 4 is a Switch 1 game, but AI-backed hackers found a way to use it in order to jailbreak Switch 2 consoles, allowing the machines to run code not approved by Nintendo. That would allow users to, in theory, run homebrew applications, but as far as I can tell, the exploit had not yet been extended to allow people to play pirated games.

Nonetheless, Nintendo acted quickly to delist SmileBasic 4 from the eShop and to update the console's firmware to address the exploit at a system level. That's a necessary step, too, as SmileBasic 4 was not the only application that could be used to exploit it. Notably, Civilization 7, a game that is available on cartridge and thus impervious to delistings and software updates, could also be used for the jailbreak.

Even so, it seems that SmileBasic 4 itself has been updated to ensure that a similar exploit never happens again. As Twitter user MarioMasta64 notes, the software itself has gotten a new update post-reinstatement aimed at closing the hole used for the jailbreak. The application's demo – which could also be used to exploit it – has also been updated, but it has not yet been relisted on the eShop.

He's been covering games (with occasional dalliances in the worlds of anime and pro wrestling) since 2015, first as a freelancer, then as a news writer at PCGamesN for nearly five years. His love for games was sparked somewhere between Metal Gear Solid 2 and Knights of the Old Republic, and these days you can usually find him splitting his entertainment time between retro gaming, the latest big action-adventure title, or a long haul in American Truck Simulator.

Ubisoft is betting big on a new AI video game development studio — and the timing of 166 layoffs raises questions

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The makers behind Assassin’s Creed are going all in on generative AI while letting human talent go

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Every time news comes out about the French video game publisher/developer Ubisoft, it’s either something gamers are pleasantly surprised by or something entirely negative that doesn’t come as a shock.

It was nice to learn that studio franchises like Assassin’s Creed, The Division and Rayman were either getting content expansions or new series installments altogether. But it was equally disappointing to learn about the 2026 cancellation of several in-development projects, including the troubled remake of Prince of Persia: The Sands of Time. Gamers were ecstatic to hear that Ubisoft halted any future development in NFTs and Web3 gaming. But now they’ll probably be less than enthused all over again, now that Ubisoft is shifting its next big tech focus to generative AI.

After releasing its latest earnings report, Ubisoft has announced its intentions to go all-in on incorporating generative AI technologies into video game development with a new studio. But with that news comes the company’s latest round of layoffs, which resulted in hundreds of its studio’s game developers losing their roles at Ubisoft.

With both events happening so close to each other, it’s easy to see why gamers are questioning Ubisoft’s latest moves.

Ubisoft made it known that they’re making a big move towards playable generative AI experiences when it highlighted the new in-house studio behind its efforts in its most recent earnings report.

“Motherbrain, the team behind Teammates, the Group’s first playable generative AI experience, has been established as a dedicated subsidiary developing proprietary technology for new forms of gameplay. Its current focus is on new player experiences, smart NPCs with memory and AI game masters that manage large-scale systemic worlds,” the company stated. “It aims to serve creators globally, both within Ubisoft’s Creative House ecosystem and beyond, and form partnerships with selected technology actors.”

Ubisoft CEO Yves Guillemot commented on the creation of Motherbrain, noting that it is “the team behind our first playable generative AI experience Teammates, as a dedicated subsidiary developing proprietary technologies for gameplay to empower creators and open up new possibilities for richer, more immersive and engaging player experiences.”

Sign up to the Tom's AI Guide weekly newsletter summing up all the biggest AI news you need to know. Plus, analysis from our AI editors and tips on how to use the latest AI tools!

Those experiences will focus on utilizing more intelligent non-playable characters with stronger memory and AI game masters that properly manage large-scale in-game worlds.

Motherbrain’s Teammates has been in development for a good while now and was recently showcased to members of the gaming media in a playable state. The people who got their hands on it refer to it as an immersive first-person shooter where players are joined by two robotic AI companions that they can order around by issuing real-time voice commands.

The game’s narrative director, Virginie Mosser, looked to ease the fears of gamers who are wary of generative AI being implemented in video games and AI tools being used to make them.

“At first, I had the same concerns as many others,” Mosser noted. “But I discovered that it’s the exact opposite of removing the human from the process. I still write the story and character personalities, but instead of fixed lines, we create these kinds of fences that let NPCs improvise within the world but stay within the boundaries of the lore and motivations we have given them. They can improvise, but we still set the rules and direct the story and characters.”

Gamers still have reason to give Ubisoft the side eye about its big generative AI video game development plans, especially since the company was so quick to abandon its trend-chasing foray into the world of NFTs and Web3 gaming. The most recent round of layoffs at the company, which resulted in the loss of 166 roles from its Montreal and Quebec studios, has also given gamers another reason to be skeptical about Ubisoft’s AI game-making initiative.

Even though Ubisoft enacted these layoffs “to ensure our resources are aligned with current production priorities and business needs,” it’s not out of the realm of possibility to question its true motives for its latest job cuts.

Has Ubisoft decided to pour major investments into generative AI game development at the cost of sacrificing its human talent? Will more layoffs occur in the near future as the company strengthens its AI game development efforts? Can gamers expect to hear about more AI tool usage implemented in the creation of future entries in Ubisoft’s biggest franchises? And if Motherbrain’s Teammates bombs, will Ubisoft quickly leave generative AI video game development behind and go on to the next big tech fad?

As someone who’s always chatting with gamers and critics who cover the medium, I always hear how they could do without any sort of AI being utilized in the video game creation process. And they especially don’t want to hear AI voices emanating from their favorite games and replacing human ones.

That last sentiment is evidenced by the overwhelmingly negative reactions to Embark Studios (the development studio behind The Finals) replacing its human esports broadcasters with in-game AI commentators. Ubisoft has experienced some major ups and downs as of late—their major bet on generative AI game development has a high chance of producing another loss if their latest endeavors don’t work as well as intended.

Elton Jones covers AI for Tom’s Guide, and tests all the latest models, from ChatGPT to Gemini to Claude to see which tools perform best — and how they can improve everyday productivity.

He is also an experienced tech writer who has covered video games, mobile devices, headsets, and now artificial intelligence for over a decade. Since 2011, his work has appeared in publications including The Christian Post, Complex, TechRadar, Heavy, and ONE37pm, with a focus on clear, practical analysis.

Today, Elton focuses on making AI more accessible by breaking down complex topics into useful, easy-to-understand insights for a wide range of readers.
