The Tutu Is the Tactile Miracle Fashion Needs Right Now

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The tutu’s fashion legacy just keeps going.

Distinctly fluffy, feminine, and over-the-top, the tutu has an unrivaled fashion legacy. Is it any wonder that in the past few weeks, celebrities have been joyfully committed to wearing the frilly staple? It may be peak Nutcracker season, but even so, Balletcore will never die. The aesthetic has experienced its original iteration (dating back to the 1800s), its first fashion wave in the ’50s and again in the ’80s, followed by a darker take, a more grounded look, and yet it still persists. At the center of it is the tutu: Nicole Kidman recently wore a Matthieu Blazy Chanel dress with a perfectly layered tutu tulle confection of a skirt that would give Carrie Bradshaw a run for her money. Elsewhere, Rosalía, Tinashe, and Olivia Rodrigo have all recently donned the tutu. The look right now reads as more punky Y2K or ’80s couture.

The spring/summer 2027 runways were littered with echoes of the tutu, too. Both Chanel and Dior showcased voluminous skirts with tutu-like shapes; Dior’s were made of cut fabric petals. Rising label Agro Studio integrated ethereal watercolor tutus into its recent collection. And it’s impossible to forget Cecilie Bahnsen’s undying dedication to the garment. Her fall/winter 2026 collection was a tribute to movement and dance and repositioned the tutu as an everyday item versus occasionwear, pairing it with fleeces and down jackets as live dancers wove throughout the venue wearing tutus. “They brought a beautiful femininity and softness to the more utilitarian pieces, creating an interesting contrast,” Bahnsen tells ELLE. “And as the dancers moved through the show, the tutus came to life, adding a wonderful sense of volume, lightness, and movement to the silhouettes.”

In 2026, wearing a tutu is an undeniable endorphin booster. Perhaps it’s deeply and satisfyingly impractical, the exact opposite of wearing a pair of denim jeans and a T-shirt or button-down, the kinds of items that typically dominate capsule wardrobes. “I love the femininity, the sculptural quality, and the beautiful bounce and movement of a tutu,” adds Bahnsen. “There is something so joyful, light, and full of positive energy about it.”

Given the tutu’s broad history, it’s no wonder vintage tutus are experiencing a bit of a revival too. According to Haile Lidow, founder of Lidow Archive, there’s been a surge in demand. “The tutus I can’t keep in stock right now are lighter and more ballerina-focused,” she tells ELLE. “I have this David Dalrymple for Patricia Field white tutu skirt that was made to be like the famous Carrie Bradshaw title sequence look, and that piece has been renting out constantly. I’ve also been obsessed with tattered tulle lately and the line it tows between innocence and darkness.”

Like Bahnsen, she sees an inherent joy in the silhouette and also links the current wide appeal to the extra textural feeling. “There’s something very tactile about tutus, and in an age of digital-everything, I think we’re craving pieces that seem to reach out and ask us to interact with them IRL,” she adds. In a season of pared-back dressing, the tutu’s frothy excess feels like a joyful rebellion.

Hasan Piker Joins VF’s Fair Game: “I Want to Prove I’m Not a Terrorist”

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One of the most prominent figures of the so-called Bluenami was not a candidate on the ballot, but rather a Twitch streamer and political commentator: Hasan Piker.

This year Hasan was involved in dozens of races across the country, campaigning for progressive candidates like Michigan senator-elect Abdul El-Sayed. But you’re probably more likely to associate Hasan’s name with what other people have had to say about him—from Fox News commentators to more established members of the Democratic Party.

Hasan regularly courts controversy on both his hours-long Twitch streams, where he’s said things like “America deserved 9/11,” and social media, where he’s written that Hamas “is a thousand times better than” Israel. He claims he’s often taken out of context, and that his audience knows what he really means—and where he stands. In his mind, he’s a leftist provocateur, maybe, but one who understands the importance of working-class values and electoral strategy.

While Hasan has walked back some of his more inflammatory language, his rhetoric raises the question: Is he a liability to the cause he champions? Or is he successfully reaching the critical audience—namely, young men—that helped elect the wave of progressives poised to take office in January?

With 2028 on the horizon, I wanted to know how Hasan thinks about his growing platform, and if he sees his role shifting in the media landscape. What responsibility does he have to his viewers and to the candidates he supports? And does he regret any of those past statements?

We also got into the DSA’s more extreme positions and how to make sense of them; why he thinks the Republicans put his thirst trap on the jumbotron at their convention; and how he, a self-proclaimed socialist, thinks about his own money.

Mark Guiducci: Hasan Piker, welcome to Fair Game.

Thanks for coming. Hasan, you are more online than just about anyone. You spend at least seven hours a day streaming. You reach tens of thousands of people at a time on average, and it’s all in the channel that you control. I’m curious, what are you doing here with me today? Why are you showing up for a show with a legacy media publication? What’s in it for you?

Well, I saw the clips and everybody looked really good. The lighting was perfect. I saw the Dari[aliza Avila Chevalier] clips, I saw Ta-Nehisi [Coates], and I was like, I got to get in on that.

Yeah. So shouts out to the team. You guys did a phenomenal job lighting this up. It looks very cool too.

But the serious answer is, I mean, I reach an audience that obviously does not watch television, does not read legacy publications at all. And I want to show people that read Vanity Fair that I am not a terrorist.

Right. Which brings me to my next question because—

Yeah, exactly. That would be the name of the episode. You’ve been everywhere this year, and you became this kind of bogeyman for the right, who probably have never heard you, watched your stream, or heard anything you’ve said out of your mouth. They’ve just heard quotes repeated by news anchors. And not even just for the right, even for some on the left. And you played this interesting role because you were a surrogate and a media figure and then a media figure covering the media as well. I want to spend a little bit of time talking about how you see your role. And to start, do you consider yourself a journalist?

Okay. Because it’s like you present this interesting new type of figure. I was thinking about how you’ve done an interview with CNN, you’ve done interviews with MS NOW, but they don’t have you on as much as I would expect them to.

I have some thoughts, but I wonder why you think that is.

I think I’ve pissed off a bunch of executives over there, because, in my experience, there are plenty of amazing reporters and journalists and producers that I know that work at all of these newsrooms, right? But from what I have seen, the masthead has a very different editorial slant than sometimes what the reporters want to cover.

They talk about me a lot. And also I’ve never been invited on any of these outlets as a pundit either, which is also strange because, as you also pointed to, I don’t just operate as a campaignster. As a matter of fact, that’s something I did very briefly and I did not like it at all. I’m first and foremost a commentator…. I do have the capacity to do journalism at times. I’ll do interviews with candidates. I’ll go out and—

Well, candidates I like. Sometimes I do interviews with candidates I don’t like or I’m unsure about as well. The problem is most of the candidates don’t want to come on if they know I’m gonna cook ’em. Like Gavin Newsom, I have had feelers out to get him on the broadcast.

I have. Gavin, Gavin, come on the show. I am a taxpaying California resident. I’m one of your constituents. You want to be president in 2028, you’re gonna have to be able to talk to people like myself.

All right. So, Hasan, this cycle you were involved in some way in, by one count, 48 different candidates’ races, and AIPAC and pro-Israel groups spent about $101 million in those races. And on Election Day, your stream was the second most watched in the country after NBC News’s “Kornacki Cam.” With a little bit of distance, how are you thinking about what happened a couple of weeks ago, both for yourself and for the country?

I feel a lot of hope. Hopefully it’s not going to be short-lived. But I feel very excited for the first time in a very long time, after 10 years of some of the most odious politics that we’ve seen from Trumpism, from MAGA. We finally saw a real sea change through electoralism. People truly have had enough with Trumpism.

I wouldn’t be so certain because I feel like if there’s one controlled opposition party that is capable of breathing life back into MAGA, it would be the establishment Democrats. So I’m a little bit worried, but it’s very clear that people are truly fed up.

All right. So there are a couple of different narratives that are coming out of the midterms right now. Rahm Emanuel, for instance, is touting this as a victory for moderate Democrats, saying that they ran 21 points above Kamala Harris. The DSA, meanwhile, is claiming that American voters have “emphatically endorsed socialism.” Other pundits are saying that these big gains have nothing to do with the Democrats and are actually about the war in Iran and high gas prices. So does this debate between moderates and progressives feel like a healthy back-and-forth to you, or does this feel like the new aisle that has to be straddled?

I mean, I think this is not even a new debate. This factional battle has existed within the Democratic Party and perhaps—

Yeah, but the progressive wing was always really fringe.

And now we’re talking about—I don’t know if 50/50 is right. I don’t want to use numbers, but—

I think it’s a testament to our success, especially in the midterms, that there are a lot of people who are trying to protect their comfortable sinecures and are outright panicking. And all of that completely sidesteps the reality that the vulgar Bernie-ism of 2016 was echoed through every single candidacy, every single campaign all across the country, from the obvious Berniecrats like Abdul El-Sayed to even people like Roy Cooper, who in the past were very moderate, very centrist. And he still is a center-right figure, which clearly works in North Carolina. But even he was railing against data centers, billionaires, the uncertainty surrounding AI. I mean, this is left populism. And that rhetoric, one could say, was deployed in the past by even the likes of Bill Clinton and Al Gore.

But for some strange reason, there was a turning point in 2016 where the Democratic Party adopted this strange role of defending the corporate class and defending the elites and took ownership over, I guess, defending these institutions overall. And these institutions are worthy of defending, for the record. However, from the perspective of the masses, if the government is failing them, if these institutions are failing them, then they wanted someone to break it. And that was obviously the right populism of Donald Trump. And break those institutions, he surely did. And we’ve seen the devastating results of that.

So you credit the progressivism with what happened on election night?

Absolutely. I mean, there’s certainly local moderation on issues, right? Because James Talarico went from saying “God is nonbinary” to “we need to defend our transgender brothers and sisters, our neighbors,” which is, again, insanely woke for Texas, or just in general, right? There are far more conservative figures in the party than James Talarico’s current position on the trans issue.

Okay. But sometimes the way that you run—oftentimes the way that you run needs to be different than the way that you govern or lead once you’re there. Do you think it is productive to continue pushing some of the farther-left ideas once they get to Congress? Or is pragmatism and getting things done, you know, defining things that are really achievable and sticking to them as a bloc, as a group, and pushing them over the line— when you’re talking to some of these winners, are you talking about [that]?

I think if I could speak to the entire incoming freshman class or the entire caucus—

I would say, be bold, be brave, and obviously immediately implement accountability. Investigations are an absolute must. This is what people are demanding. After the economy, the second concern, the biggest concern, is corruption. The second term with Trump has been unbelievable. I mean, look, I’m Turkish. I’ve seen corruption in the Turkish government. I’ve seen corruption and tracked it and talked about it all around the world with different governments. I’ve never seen anything like what the Trump administration has done because of how flagrant and in your face it was. And I think people responded to that, finally, in the appropriate way they should, with anger.

So you’re calling for subpoenas and investigations.

I think impeachment, especially for Trump, is probably the last thing that I care about. I mean, I want these guys to go to jail. Let’s be clear. If they’ve done the crime, they need to do the time. I don’t think anyone is above the law. We have to restore law and order. So I’m a law-and-order Democrat. And I know that sounds something very different in Washington, DC, or in general. I think when politicians say that, they mean, like, punish the poor for minor crimes. I’m talking about punishing the wealthy for their corruption, for their major crimes, because they’re the ones who are robbing people blind.

But, you know, a lot of people would say that impeachment would be a distraction. So I guess what I’m saying is, if you had to choose three agenda items that the Democrats are agreeing on and they’re going forward with—you already said subpoenas and investigations.

What else? What are the other two that in the first year you really want to see happen?

I think it’s virtually impossible [with] the current DOJ to—they’re not impartial, right? I don’t think that we are going to move the needle on actual criminal investigations before the Democrats win the White House in 2028. But all of this is building up for that moment. And for that reason, I think we need to talk about affordability—and not just simply talk about affordability, but also explain how we’re going to solve the affordability crisis through a comprehensive legislative agenda. And one of the major issues around affordability that is oftentimes discussed as a separate thing, but it’s not, is health care. And the Democratic Party’s approach to health care has been lackluster. Even though Americans obviously overwhelmingly trust the Democrats with fixing health care or solving the problem with health care, the reality of the matter is, while the ACA was a fantastic improvement to what existed previously, it’s not enough, and we have to move the needle in a healthier direction. We have to push for a single-payer health care solution.

I’m perfectly fine with that. I’ve joked about that for years ’cause he’s so invested. He’s such a petty despot and so narcissistic that—

Yes. I have pitched this over and over again on my show. Look, you win so big with Donald Trump if you go to Donald Trump and you say, “Look, Obamacare, not the best, right? Now you get to destroy Obamacare and you get to build something else in your name. We call it Trumpcare. It’s single-payer. It’s Medicare for All.” I think he would do it.

All right, so let’s take a step back. You were talking a second ago about how Talarico was woke. And on November 4, Twitter, formerly known as X, was amazing. Memes, jokes, you know.

I see what you did there. Yeah. We flipped it back.

Yeah, we flipped it back. With the Democrats’ victory, the idea that woke was back, do you think that’s true? Do you think that we’re going to see, culturally, the return of this idea of woke? And do you think that’s a good thing for the Democrats?

Yes. I do think that the pendulum swing was an inevitability. I talked about this quite a bit. I saw the early warning signs when [Joe] Biden won. That was one of my major warnings that young men were going to go back to the Gamergate era of very far-right politics because the Biden administration was not delivering. And what ended up happening with this anti-woke narrative, this culture war that the Republicans had seemingly won, that they presented as a mandate, right, was some of the most odious commentary, some of the most vile rhetoric that anyone had ever seen.

Definitely. The idea of woke now, though, I'm just curious—being politically correct or precise in one’s language was, for a time, an outward indication of being a progressive or maybe even being a good person in culture. Do you think that’s still the case?

I mean, I’m progressive, so I think so, but I’m a little biased. But I will say this: I think that there were certainly social excesses, which is ironic because this whole Woke 1.0, 2.0 distinction, I feel a little responsible for in the discourse because I jokingly used to say—

Yeah, now I’ve joked about that too, and now journalists are reaching out to me being like, “Do you think Woke 3.0 is real?” I’m like, “I’m joking, guys. It’s not serious.” But here’s the thing, there is a little bit of sincerity there. I think one of the things that I routinely criticized during that 2016-to-2022, 2023, era of, I guess, Woke 1.0 was the social excesses, the maximalist demands, the inability to understand that people are complex and don’t have the same lived experience that everybody else has. I always would get so frustrated because we on the left say America’s a white supremacist country, and yet we don’t ever reflect on what kind of social conditioning comes along with that. So that if you do actually hear someone engage in a microaggression, for example, that we have to be a little bit more understanding and try to ease them into the conversation.

And on the other side, people will say, “Oh, that’s normalizing this kind of behavior. It’s unacceptable.” And I totally see the other side of that argument as well, but I think, especially if you’re campaigning or especially if you’re building a movement, you do have to be a little bit more understanding and not be so hardheaded and assume that everyone has to agree with you from the jump without you making a convincing case as to why you might be correct.

Okay. All right. So we’ve talked a lot about what you think about the political landscape, but I want to talk also about your role in it right now and going forward. So initially, you were called the Joe Rogan of the left. Recently, you’ve said you operate more like a Charlie Kirk. Is that the right analog for the kind of influence you want to have? And if not, is there another?

I don’t think there is an appropriate analog because I’m everything all at once at the same time, because I work out and I do collaborations with people who are not expressly political, so that’s kind of like Joe Rogan. I go out and operate sometimes as a campaign surrogate, and I do rallies at college campuses and I deliver speeches, so that’s kind of like Charlie Kirk. But then usually my day-to-day commentary is what I like to jokingly refer to as a Rush Limbaugh for Zoomers. So I do all of those things at the same time, and that’s, I think, part of the reason why it’s so hard to make sense of what I do from the perspective of someone who just doesn’t understand Twitch or doesn’t understand exactly what I’m doing and has only seen negatively framed clips or clips out of context, that kind of thing. Having said that, on the political-ideological side of things, of course I am the furthest from all of those figures. I’m just talking about the form and function of it all.

Okay. So it seems like you’ve been recently the guy that progressives come to when they’re trying to run a primary from the left, but then when they get to the general or to office, maybe they don’t need you as much, or they have left you behind, or they distanced themselves from you. And I think about [Zohran] Mamdani in this case. I just—

Maybe because of that Rush Limbaugh–esque heightened rhetoric; do you agree with that characterization? And how does that make you feel?

Well, my feelings are immaterial in this. As long as they’re delivering, I think that’s what matters. I’ve said this before: I love Zohran and, off the rip, my attitude on this was always just put the policies in the bag. If you’re delivering for the people of New York, and if you’re delivering successfully and every success that you have as a democratic socialist mayor of New York bodes well for the rest of this movement, then I’m happy.

Yeah, but are you talking to him behind the scenes? There’s still a relationship there? Because honestly—

Yes. But the thing is, what I’m doing is too new for people to fully realize, to fully comprehend. So of course there’s going to be some growing pains as far as how to navigate through an unbelievably contentious media environment, especially if the candidate[s] themselves are Muslim. And, you know, I think that is the reason why—the two major figures that I can point to that have done a little bit of distancing, at least in rhetoric, even though they never disavow the platform, disavow the person, they just say, “Oh, I don’t agree with that statement”—those two figures are Abdul El-Sayed and Zohran Mamdani. They’re both very progressive. One’s a democratic socialist, the other one’s not, but they’re still very progressive for American politics, and they’re Muslim. I think that is a big part of the reason why I’ve given them a lot more bandwidth to operate in this space in whichever way they want to, because the expectation or the fears that they have are very real. As someone who has been at the center of a lot of this manufactured outrage, a lot of this controversy, being presented as an Islamist, jihadist at times, a terrorist, I know the role that the first Muslim senator has to play on the campaign trail. So on that front, of course they got to do what they got to do.

So you intellectually understand it, but do you feel used?

I would only feel that way if they did not follow through on their promises that they made on the campaign trail. If Abdul came out tomorrow and was like, “Money out of politics? Maybe money in politics. Money in your pocket? Maybe you don’t get money in your pocket. Medicare for All? Medicare for some”—if he were to just flip the script on not just me, but Michiganders and America as a whole, then I would feel very betrayed.

So we talked a little bit earlier about your audience at the very beginning. You know, you’re an interesting figure for them. There are a significant number of them that are with you for hours on end a day, and I think they probably look up to you. What responsibility do you feel to the people who watch your streams?

As far as the responsibilities that I have, I am very aware of them. I see myself as, in some respects, NPR for Zoomers. For many people, and now even an older audience as well, I am their primary news outlet. They have me on in the background, and I’m offering news and commentary throughout the day while they’re going through and doing their chores or at work with me blasting in one ear. So I know that if I were to stop randomly, people would feel it, because I see it as—I listen to The Daily; I listen to NPR every morning. If somehow I wake up one morning and NPR is no longer on, I’d be like, What happened? How am I gonna figure out what’s going on in the world?

Yeah, that’s just a lot of pressure, though, because NPR has a whole staff of people that, [if] someone’s sick, someone can pinch-hit.

I mean, I’m fine living in that environment. I’m happy to do it. I’m happy to take on that responsibility.

Can I ask, what’s your personal life like right now? I mean, that is a lot of time on the air, and probably you have to read the news yourself too. So are you seeing friends a lot? Do you see your family a lot?

I keep my private life private for obvious reasons ’cause there are a lot of insane people out there. But yes, I do spend a good deal of time with friends and family. I have normie friends as well that are just completely removed from the world of politics and from the world of Hollywood, influencers, that kind of thing.

Yeah, no, they keep me grounded, for sure, and my family certainly does as well. I spend every waking moment that I’m not on camera doing things like that, you know, enjoying the finer things in life.

Do you think of yourself as a role model?

In some respects, yes. I don’t think people should follow everything that I do to a T, but I definitely do see that. I recognize the responsibility that influencers have over so many people, and I try to put my best foot forward in an effort to cultivate a nicer space on the internet, one that is more productive for people. I try to urge people to go out and unionize in their workplaces, join the Democratic Socialists of America, but then also at the same time, I try to get them to go out and work out. I’m very open about my fitness journey, my health journey. And the same even goes for my presentation. I’ve gone through these different eras now on my broadcast. Right now we’re in the suit arc. And then I sometimes see that reflected in the crowd as well when I go out to these rallies. I start seeing people also wearing suits, which is a little strange to experience, but it’s not the worst thing, right?

Yeah, I think that is a good influence. You’re rocking a nice suit yourself.

I bought this at a photo shoot for Days magazine. This stylist had pulled this and I loved it.

I literally was like, “I’m buying this from you.” And then since then, she’s been pulling stuff for me.

Can I ask, are you having fun? Because it seems like you are, but then what we were talking about before, with the way that you might feel abandoned—and especially the past few months, this cycle, has it been fun?

No, it has not been fun. I don’t think it’s great to be called a terrorist and lied about and slandered endlessly with the hopes that this could potentially tank the Democratic Party’s chances of bringing about accountability to this fascist administration. I did not enjoy that.

And I certainly also did not enjoy the fact that I was kind of left out on my own a little bit, where—of course I got a lot of support from the movement, DSA, and the wonderful slate of candidates that we worked with, but I’ve never had institutional backing. And as a matter of fact, there was a lot of anxiety from the institution that is the Democratic Party. So all of its loyal servants were going on TV and being like, “He’s a horrible person who needs to go away,” over and over again. So that didn’t feel great. ’Cause there’s always negative polarization when Trump attacks someone and the Republicans attack someone, but if there isn’t any backing from the liberal side, even from a partisan perspective, then that complicates things. All of a sudden you’re like, Okay, well, who’s on my side now? I have nobody to push back.

You’re playing this game where you try to find the worst quotes that you can and frame them as negatively as you can, and then attack candidates that might not even know the context behind some of these words. Bernie was, I think, the best, ironically enough, at dealing with it when he was asked by Abby Phillip. He called me a radio man, which is funny. He’s been on the show, still doesn’t know what I do, which is fine. That’s unc, that’s grand-unc. But he basically said, “Look, everybody says silly things. I mean, he talks for thousands of hours. He’s a radio man. And not only does everybody say silly things, but ultimately there’s a reason why he has such a passionate fan base, because we agree on the broad spectrum of issues that he advocates for, and so do all of his fans. So to reduce someone to a couple of choice clips is obviously going to never give you the full picture.” I mean, he didn’t use all those words.

It’s not about being a person; it’s about being a cudgel, just something that you can use as a counterbalance.

I don’t think liberals, and even the left broadly, [are] ready for how to deal with that because we’ve never really had a person like me in the past. I mean, the Republicans obviously have three to four decades of this, coming from William F. Buckley all the way to Rush Limbaugh and Charlie Kirk and numerous other far-right figures, that they know how to brush this aside. I mean, JD Vance, when asked about his affiliation with Tucker Carlson, will be like, “He’s my friend.” And that’s it. That’s that.

Okay. So you’re streaming most of the day, you’re reacting to news in real time, and just even by your own admission, you’ve said some—I think you said “silly things.” I would say some pretty extreme things along the way.

And I know you’ve walked back past statements. “America deserved 9/11”—how many times have you heard that this season? Quote: “If you cared about Medicare fraud, you’d kill Rick Scott.” You’ve said things that you wish you had said differently. Do you regret anything that you’ve said? Because I think regret is different than “wish I said differently.”

It’s a tough one. I mean, look, I probably will regret things that I will say in the future as well. It’s virtually impossible for me not to say things—

Yeah, it is, but it also isn’t because I have apologized and taken back some of the things that I’ve said and apologized for how I worded or framed things. But do I regret it? I mean, I feel like I’m stronger as a consequence of all of those experiences at the same time, so I don’t know.

Do you feel like someone in your role has a different responsibility with their words or their public platform than an elected official or even a journalist?

Okay. But you also said that you are a role model.

Yeah, absolutely. It goes back to the space that I occupy and what I try to do with it. Right? I don’t think that I am, at least to my audience or anyone else that’s paying attention, doing anything that’s remotely politically as toxic as the way that the current systems operate. The rude behavior that I demonstrate is what people get frustrated by or what they attack, but—

Yeah, I am vulgar, for sure. But I think the systems of violence and oppression that people are subjected to [are] far worse than any vulgar thing I could have ever said. And we kind of saw this dynamic play itself out on the campaign trail as well. And I think the finality of it all came with Haley Stevens, when she decided to lean into the “Hasan Piker is such a problem that I'm leaving the Democratic Party and endorsing the Republican platform.” [That] was, I think in some respects, a wake-up call because it forced everyone to go, Wait a minute. No, those guys are actually far worse. What are we talking about? Right? Because at the end of the day, Donald Trump is not only vulgar, but he’s also violent. And sure, I’m vulgar, but ultimately I’m fighting for social justice. I’m fighting for people who are dispossessed, people who don’t get heard at all. I’m trying to offer them a voice, a platform for their frustrations, and communicate their desires, their anxieties.

Do you think the vulgarity actually helps you with your audience? Because if they’re young men, men in their 20s, it’s like the edgelord vernacular.

Certainly, but I don’t do it for some tactical purpose. I do it because that’s just who I am.

I mean, I always say, look, if you go after marginalized people, vulnerable populations, and you bully them, I’m gonna bully you. Someone has to do it.

For what it’s worth, you’re being very civil right now.

Oh yeah. I mean, this is a very different format. I am very different in these sorts of professional settings than I am on my own broadcast where I’m offering off-the-cuff commentary on these hot-button issues.

I’m much more civil here. I’m not going to talk in the same way that I do on my broadcast. But here’s the thing, I can’t be fake on my broadcast. And I’m not even saying I’m being fake here. I’m just being nice.

But what I will say is this: A lot of people don’t realize that this is an interactive medium. Most people think, when they see a clip, especially if it’s chopped a certain way—

Yes. Most people think this is my preproduced, prescripted statement ’cause that’s all they’ve seen from the Jake Tappers of the world. They don’t realize that I am offering extemporaneous commentary on a breaking news story in real time, without a production team, while simultaneously—

No. While simultaneously duking it out with anonymous accounts. What is the one thing that everybody hates on Twitter and everywhere else on the internet?

The comment section. My entire platform revolves around yelling at the comment section, and the comment section is yelling back at me.

But that is like, I’m in the zone. That’s when I’m in the pocket. I love being in that space.

You’ve been outspoken about distinguishing criticism of Israel from antisemitism, but do you think that there are moments when legitimate anger at Israel can create an environment in which antisemitism becomes acceptable, even among people who would never consider themselves antisemitic?

And what responsibility do you think you have, given the size of your audience and your influence with it, to call that out when it happens?

I do call it out when it happens, and it happens quite frequently. It’s not a secret that antisemitism has exploded in the Western world, especially after October 7. And that is why I take special care and consideration when talking about this issue in particular. Whenever I talk about Israel, there’s always going to be some people who will be like, “Well, you know how the Jews are.” And I will always take the time out to explain to my audience, and to whoever’s saying an offensive statement or an antisemitic statement, why they’re wrong. And I think it’s important to do that, not because—it’s not like the canary mission of the ADL is gonna be like, “This guy that we have presented to you as a prominent critic of Israel, that we’re desperately trying to paint as an antisemite, has actually spent thousands of hours combating antisemitism and will continue to do so because he’s an antifascist.” They don’t want to present the complexity of that argument because, let’s be real, it’s pretty tough trying to defend Israel. It’s not an easy endeavor.

Can you think of a time when you contributed to that problem, not intentionally, but maybe through something you said and the way your audience responded?

No, because every clip that people post, or every moment that people have posted to point to this suspected antisemitism that I harbor, oftentimes is sandwiched between a comment that I have made leading up to it that completely destroys the narrative. The example I will present to you is the famous moment where I got disavowed by the entire Democratic Party. I said something that I have said as an anti-Zionist for the last decade and will continue to say because it’s the truth: that Jews are not monolithic and that Israel should not be the responsibility of the entire Jewish population, and that anti-Zionism is not antisemitism; however, that forced conflation of Jews in Israel is increasing antisemitism. And they chopped it up and packaged it and turned it into this major media moment.

So speaking of misinterpretations, you recently filed a defamation lawsuit against conservative commentator Scott Jennings, although you’ve said you were very deliberately anti-litigation for a long time before that. So what changed? Why this suit? And who else are you planning to sue?

So what changed is the heinous nature of this defamation. It was definitely a bridge too far. I feel like there is a boundary that was crossed that I could no longer abide by. And there’s a level of defamation and slander that takes place in the political conversation, but what Scott Jennings did was so disgusting and so malicious that I had to demand a retraction. Snopes did an article immediately because it was so heinous. He claimed that I was on video advocating to lynch Black voters. I’ve spent my entire life fighting against white supremacy, and the fact that they maliciously edited this video to make it seem like I was in favor of something that I’ve always been in opposition to, something so heinous, a hate crime, was outright unacceptable. So I wish that it didn’t have to get to litigation, but for some strange reason, they decided not to respond to our request for a retraction and now we’re here.

I mean, there are a lot of people who have engaged in that exact same kind of defamation and slander. So whoever has participated in it should probably be on the lookout.

Okay. In the national chapter of the DSA, there are some pretty extreme platform positions that—I saw Mamdani answer these questions on Fox News the other night. I’m sure you did too.

And I'm actually just curious where you stand on some of them.

So here’s the thing with the platform in its entirety. I think the format itself doesn’t actually do justice to the program, to describe the program, and it’s almost impossible to explain it as an elected representative or a mayor in a hostile or contentious media environment, if you’re on Fox News or whatever. The issue, I think, with the program is, there are certain elements of it that I don’t agree with fundamentally, but it’s a democratically organized, democratically voted-on program that DSA members voted for. And the way I see it is, these are very, very long-term goals, and I think this is not talked about in mainstream media at all. It is a platform, but it’s more so a North Star that we are working toward. Designing society in a way that yields egalitarian outcomes. That we do away with certain systems of cruelty is an honorable goal, is a just goal, even if I don’t fully agree with some elements of it.

I’m not a prison abolitionist; I’m not a police abolitionist. The reason why I don’t believe in that concept is because I think that these are necessary functions that must exist, but also must serve the people. Right now it doesn’t. It serves capital and that needs to change, that needs to be retooled. However, I also recognize that the current system that exists is far more ruthless, far more insane, even if we’ve normalized it, than even police abolition is. So from my perspective, if we look at where we’re at, the carceral state that exists, where America has 25% of the entire planet’s prisoner population despite only making up 4% of the overall population, and the recidivism rates that we have in this country, and the fact that we even have a private prison system in this country—that’s insane. That is insane by global standards. So it’s tough for me to sit here and say I oppose the DSA platform and program when I understand what they are trying to communicate.

And also, I think what is the regular order of business right now is far more insane than even prison abolition. Even though prison abolition is far-fetched and I don’t agree with it, I think the best possible way to go about it would be to have an electoral program, the perceivable, the permissible, the possible, and then the long-term goals, the long-term agenda, ’cause it’s not going to be easy to run and try to win on a platform that is not only idealistic, but also does not reflect the current societal understandings of concepts. Because most people think cops are awesome, they can do no wrong, they’re heroes, they’re constantly fighting crime, and they’re stopping rapes from happening, and they’re diving in front of bullets, but that’s not the case. I wish that was the case, but that’s not the case. But people overall [have] a very positive attitude about policing as an institution.

But there’s also quick and quippy ways to respond fast. I just told you I’m a law-and-order Democrat, right?

Immediately that signals something. Another thing that I always say about policing is, I want police to do their jobs. I think that, by and large, they have a profoundly important task that they have to accomplish. Their presence is supposed to invoke law and order. Their presence sometimes invokes lawlessness and disorder. That’s unacceptable. They’re very well paid. They have the best unions. Do your jobs better. That’s my attitude on policing. So it’s antagonistic, but it’s also a demand that I’m making from this institution that I truly do believe is important for society to function. That’s one way to tackle this issue in a way that Americans understand. I like to call it chudifying your commentary. Zohran does a very good job of libifying his commentary. He’s very liberal-appealing. I like to do the opposite. I like to chudify it. I appeal to the chuds. I speak from that tone and that language when I advocate for certain things.

Yeah, edgelord vernacular, sure. But it’s kind of like, whether we’re talking about abortions or whether we’re talking about transgender bills, like anti-trans bills, I’m like, I don’t want the fucking government getting involved in these affairs. What do you want? You want some creepy pervert to do penis inspection day at your high school? What are you, a pervert? Are you a pedophile? I want small government when it comes to this kind of stuff. And that is a way to argue from a perspective that I think right-wingers can understand.

Switching gears, we talked a little bit about suits for a second, but I want to talk about style more largely. So at the RNC’s midterm convention in September, there was a 25-foot selfie of you in underwear displayed on the jumbotron. How do you think about that?

It’s a strange thing because I wish I was as in shape right now as I was, because that was peak.

Thank you. But also at the same time, I’m like, Oh, I wish I looked like that right now.

Why do you think they’re so obsessed with your looks? What is going on there? How is that helpful to them actually?

It’s not. I think about that quite a bit. I think…they’re gay.

That’s it. I think they’re in the closet. There are a lot of Republican operatives that are just either gay or in the closet and haven’t been able to figure out how to communicate their desires. So they were just frustrated and confused. But there’s no other reason to have this pathological, this psychosexual, obsession where you’re like, “My opponent, this political opponent, is a dangerous guy. He’s a radical communist, Islamist, jihadist.”

“And he’s also sexy. Look at him shirtless. Look at him shirtless.” Because I thought these guys were peak masculinity. I thought that’s what they were about. The Joe Rogan Experience, you know, Brazilian jiujitsu, and working out was supposed to be the thing in the right.

There’s nothing unmasculine about being gay.

I’m kidding. That’s not how they think of it. I know.

And not only that, but also, ultimately, why are they showing a shirtless photo of me where I look jacked?

Yeah. Okay. But it’s not just the GOP. Almost any coverage of you, including now this, includes a reference to your appearance. And I’m sorry we’re doing this right now.

Do you like it in some regard, or does it bother you?

It’s attention. I like it. In the past, when people would approach me and be like, “Why is The New York Times only talking about how you look and not talking about your ideas?” I was like, “Look, that is terrorism insurance.” If I’m getting into these legacy publishers and not being called a terrorist and instead being called a hot guy, I’ll take it.

Okay. Okay. One last question about your looks. If Calvin Klein calls tomorrow and wants you to do the Houston Street billboard, are you doing it?

I would need to immediately get on a steroid regimen. I would need to look like Kumail Nanjiani before.

I mean, yeah, I would do it. Why wouldn’t I do it?

Okay. I wonder, with your audience, because there are a bunch of young guys looking up to a guy who’s maybe slightly older than them, looks like they might want to look, is that homoerotic at all?

But I do have a fairly queer community as well. We do a census. We do a yearly census. Fourteen percent of my audience is trans, which is obviously much higher than the American population.

What are some other demos that would be interesting that we wouldn’t expect?

Well, the classic ones are obviously—it’s like 60% white men under the age of 35.

You perform best with all the people that Vanity Fair performs worst [with].

I think a lot of mainstream outlets are really bad at captivating the attention of young men in particular. That’s probably part of the reason why people are always fascinated with this group. They’re trying to understand it.

What about you captured them? How did you do it?

I was a young man at some point, and I also have the same interests, and I think that’s part of it. And I also speak—going back to the crude, the language that I deploy, the vulgar language that I deploy, it’s like I speak like a normal person. I speak and get angry in the same exact ways that normal people do.

All right. Hasan, what’s your attitude toward money? How do you think about money?

For me personally, it has a corrosive influence, I think. It has the capacity to make you unbelievably out of touch. It’s something that I think about quite a bit in my own personal experience. I was a very stubborn person with air travel. Even when I had a little bit of additional income, I refused to pay the extra for the economy-plus seats.

I’m a big dude. I’m 6’4”, I can’t fit in the seats, and I would still. It’s a point of pride. Until one day I was put in a business class seat for some kind of business travel. And at that point I had enough money to purchase business class seats on my own, and I was like, I’m never looking back.

It ruined me. It completely ruined me. So now—

Tina Brown says that the private plane ruins people, that when you see how that works, it’s over. Hook, line, sinker, people are done.

That corruption is almost—she doesn't say this, but I’m interpreting that it’s almost inevitable after that.

Yes. I think that every instance of financial stability or financial freedom that I’ve gained has made me more out of step with the struggles that I used to experience and the struggle that everyday people experience. And I think you have to always be in touch. Especially in my line of work, you have to understand where people are. On the other hand, though, it is editorial freedom, because I have been able to maintain complete editorial control, complete editorial freedom, because I am completely unbought, completely unbossed, completely unshackled. There have been numerous instances where I’ve obviously had contracts with Amazon and stuff like that because I know I need to play ball also to make sure that I am a reliable team player so they don’t deplatform me. However, there have been so many instances where people have come to me with tremendous sponsorship opportunities.

Polymarket is one that I talk about all the time. [For] 2024 election night, their opening salvo was a million dollars just to have their ticker on the side. I said, of course, no, because I feel like it’s deeply unethical to be sponsored by a gambling company, a prediction market, while, you know, you’re engaging in news commentary. But I know that if I did not have that economic freedom, that financial freedom, I would’ve probably had to think about whether or not I’d make this ethical compromise.

Calvin Klein’s fine. I mean, I take sponsorships every now and then, like gaming companies and stuff like that. If Rockstar came to me and were like, “Grand Theft Auto VI is coming out. We want to sponsor a stream,” I would fucking love that.

Some people have a number that’s like their retirement number, at which point that’s all the money they need. They close up shop and call it a day. I wonder if you have a number after which you think it would be unethical to accumulate any more. What is the maximum amount of net worth or income that you could generate after which you would feel it’s morally reprehensible?

Great question. I have no idea. I’ve never thought about it like that because there’s no world in which I will reach that number. But all the revenue that I generate goes immediately back into this broader project. It allows me to be able to travel around the country. It allows me to be able to hire people on a moment’s notice if necessary. So that’s the way I see it. I don’t have an unethical number, but I think to each their own, because I don’t want people to hear me say this and then be like, “Well, you know, you’ve collaborated with Saikat Chakrabarti. He’s a centimillionaire. You’ve talked to Tom Steyer. He’s a billionaire.” I care more about what people’s actual advocacy is and if they’re real about it or not. And I think how much they pay into certain things can show that, for sure. But anything past the point of having—not your net worth, right, but anything past the point of just having $10 million, I feel like is insane. It’s like, What are you doing? Does that make sense?

Because you said you differentiate with net worth.

I differentiate it with net worth because everyone will say, “Oh, well, I have assets. I have stocks,” or whatever. But in general, if you have—

The capacity to deploy—I feel like even $10 million is too much. If you have the capacity to deploy a million dollars into something, you’re done, you’re set. I think everything past that point is outright ridiculous.

All right. I want to look ahead to the future because 2028 is not far away, and I think all signs would show that you’re going to be a character in that cycle. So here’s the inevitable 2028 question: Do you think the public has met the 2028 candidate that you’ll champion yet, or do you think that person is still waiting in the wings somewhere?

I mean, the top answer, obviously, since we’re doing this will-they-won’t-they thing, is AOC. Right? She is the one figure, I think, on the left that has the capacity to unite the base, and she’s competent, she’s charismatic, incredible progressive track record as a legislator, and—

Well, I think all that stuff is—it’s pragmatism. I don’t think that means that she is going to come out and be like, “I am no longer in favor of the same comprehensive policy agenda that I’ve sought to advance my whole life.”

But it might mean that she wants to unite the base.

Yeah. But I don’t think you necessarily have to do compromise on the legislative front to unite the base. If I was being fully transparent, fully honest, or maybe a little harsh, my criticisms around AOC would probably be that she’s not aggressive enough and that at times she’s come across as perhaps a little too liberal, too woke, on social issues in a way that is not appealing to broad subsets of the base.

What do you plan to do? Do you urge your audience to get behind them?

I don’t know. I don’t know what I would do at that point. I haven’t even thought about it because I don’t think I need to think about it.

I don’t think it’s even going to be a problem.

I’m gonna do what I did in the primaries and the midterm broadly, and hopefully we’ll have a slate of hundreds of democratic socialists that will be running in every district that will be bold, that will excite the base, and that will chart a different path forward for the Democratic Party by force.

So I say, if this is considered too toxic for politicians to take advantage of this platform, then they’ll lose. Because my goal is not to change myself. My goal is to change politics. I want American politics to be more responsive, not only to our demands, but also reflective of our anger as well. I truly believe that we are in the moral majority. People who look at the current system and say, “This is not helping,” people who look at their insurance premiums going up and say, “We need to change this,” that is a morally righteous fury that people feel, and there is a great deal of time and effort spent on mainstream outlets, legacy publishers in the media broadly, to try to quiet that frustration. I believe that I have a more honest approach to the matter, and that is precisely the reason why my audience continues to grow as their audience continues to age out or diminish.

Oh, there’s so many directions I can go with this. Do I joke? Do I fib? Do I say what I honestly think?

Well, on my broadcast, I joke and I call him the “sex panther” because he’s so uncharismatic, but I joke and say, “Oh my God, he’s so hot. Russia is developing a nuclear space weapon, US intelligence report finds

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"A space-based capability such as this could cause rapid and sudden loss or degradation of thousands of satellites operated by countries and companies around the world."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Last month, the U.S. Defense Intelligence Agency (DIA) released a chilling and info-packed report judging the scope and breadth of threats posed by the nation's adversaries in space.

The new report focuses on China, Russia and the emerging space powers of Iran and North Korea. It looks at what's happening in Earth orbit and beyond, including cislunar space, the region between our planet and the moon.

The report — the 2026 edition of "Challenges to Security in Space" — is the third iteration of the DIA's unclassified intelligence assessment on space-related challenges and threats to U.S. national security, as well as the extent to which space is treated internationally as a warfighting domain.

Information within the report, which you can read here, is current as of July 2026. DIA's mission is to provide intelligence on foreign militaries to help prevent wars, and win the ones that are fought.

The report's up-front finding is that China, Russia, Iran, and North Korea are developing, testing, deploying and using space capabilities "in a way that threatens the security of all space systems and their associated space benefits."

Indeed, China and Russia are developing "a formidable spectrum" of offensive capabilities to degrade or destroy space services of other countries, from kinetic destruction to sophisticated jamming and cyber operations.

The DIA report also notes that "the production of cheaper, smaller, and simpler satellites, which are faster to develop and deploy, along with the expansion of commercially available launch services, are driving the rapid integration of commercial space alternatives to support national security."

While space commercialization is making previously government-centered space services widely available to interested parties, the DIA report adds, that availability also includes state and non-state actors with malign interests.

A major item dealt with by the DIA is "counterspace" — and it's not the kind that you'll find in your kitchen.

Counterspace operations can be either offensive or defensive. They can be used on purpose to reversibly or irreversibly deny, disrupt, degrade, damage or destroy a space object or component of a space system.

Here are the key elements of counterspace, as identified within the DIA report:

The DIA report underscores that Russia is developing a satellite designed to carry a nuclear weapon.

Russia is doing so despite its obligation to comply with the 1967 Outer Space Treaty. That U.N. agreement prohibits the placement of any objects carrying nuclear weapons, or any other kinds of weapons of mass destruction, into orbit around Earth.

"A space-based capability such as this could cause rapid and sudden loss or degradation of thousands of satellites operated by countries and companies around the world," the DIA report states, "as well as to the vital communications, navigation, scientific, meteorological, commercial and national security services they provide."

A nuclear detonation in space could be tailored to impact satellites in most orbits, the DIA report warns, from low Earth orbit for commercial and reconnaissance satellites, geosynchronous orbit for critical communications, and medium Earth orbit for navigation and timing satellites.

The newly issued DIA report also observes that exploring cislunar space is of increasing interest to spacefaring nations for potential scientific and economic opportunities. However, nations such as China "may be exploring military applications from cislunar space," the report notes.

China has stated its goal of developing a cislunar space economy by 2045.

"China is also the first country to attempt sustained operations on the far side of the moon, with numerous projects slated to support that effort through the next decade. China portrays its interest in cislunar space as peaceful, but PRC [People's Republic of China] officials have also discussed pursuing military capabilities in cislunar space," the report states.

Placing a keen eye on the DIA report is Peter Garretson, a senior fellow in defense studies at the American Foreign Policy Council and a strategy consultant who focuses on space and defense.

According to Garretson, the most significant positive change from earlier DIA appraisals is the explicit assessment that Russia is developing a satellite designed to carry a nuclear weapon. He told Space.com that the DIA's expanded treatment of the threat of a nuclear weapon on orbit reflects concerns in the Congress and the Trump White House.

However, Garretson also flagged what he said is a consequential omission in the report: the disappearance of the prior treatment (in the 2022 version of the report) of research competition, surveillance beyond geosynchronous Earth orbit and the potential military use of deep space for staging reserve forces.

In Garretson's view, the DIA needs to help put the puzzle pieces together for Congress and the attentive public about what exactly China is doing, and how it fits together in the nation's strategy to achieve comprehensive space dominance.

According to Garretson, "everything they are doing, from launch cadence, reusable launch, commercial space investment, investments in power, propulsion, repair, manufacturing, to commercial assets working together" supports both an economic expansion toward new resources and what China hopes will be overwhelming military power and strategic depth.

Garretson senses the moves by China to develop extractive and in-space industries "is truly the strategic threat that could crush American space power. Now is the time to be making the investments to ensure we have a 'freedom's forge' and 'arsenal of democracy' in space that can protect the open space commerce that is the nation's strategy."

As for the DIA's outlook for the future: The agency concluded that space almost certainly will remain an increasingly competitive and crowded environment as space capabilities improve and other countries seize opportunities in the final frontier.

"The increase of Chinese, Russian, Iranian and North Korean counterspace capabilities, rapid growth in the number of space objects, and the proliferation of requirements for space-enabled services," the DIA report states, "will all complicate operations in space in the coming decade."

Leonard David is an award-winning space journalist who has been reporting on space activities for more than 50 years. Currently writing as Space.com's Space Insider Columnist among his other projects, Leonard has authored numerous books on space exploration, Mars missions and more, with his latest being "Moon Rush: The New Space Race" published in 2019 by National Geographic. He also wrote "Mars: Our Future on the Red Planet" released in 2016 by National Geographic. Leonard  has served as a correspondent for SpaceNews, Scientific American and Aerospace America for the AIAA. He has received many awards, including the first Ordway Award for Sustained Excellence in Spaceflight History in 2015 at the AAS Wernher von Braun Memorial Symposium. You can find out Leonard's latest project at his website and on Twitter.

Gemini Notebook's New Interactive Site Builder Is Much More Useful Than I Expected

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

David Nield is a technology journalist from Manchester in the U.K. who has been writing about gadgets and apps for more than 20 years.

He has a bachelor's degree in English Literature from Durham University, where he also spent a term as editor of the award-winning student newspaper Palatinate. His journalism career started in print media, where he contributed to and edited several technology magazines and bookazines sold in the U.K. and internationally. More recently, he has worked as a freelancer for some of the biggest technology publications on the web, covering everything from on-the-ground reporting about product launches, to detailed explainers and how-to guides on apps, gadgets, and platforms. His expertise covers broad areas of consumer tech, including smartphones, laptops, wearables, and AI.

Gemini Notebook, Google's AI tool for study and research, already lets you do a lot with your notes: You can turn them into flashcard quizzes, interactive podcasts, video explainers, mind maps, and infographics, as well as interrogating the sources you've collected through AI-powered prompts and responses. Now there's something new. You can also build interactive websites from your notebooks.

That might not immediately seem like the most obvious fit, turning your research notes into a mass of HTML code. But it gives you another way to look at your material and to get insights from it. Here's how it works, what you could potentially do with it, and how impressively it worked when I put it to the test on some early 20th-century Irish literature.

Website building is rolling out now for Gemini Notebook's web app for all users, and is "coming soon" to the mobile app.  You won't see a website option in the Studio panel alongside Audio Overview, Reports, Quiz, and the rest; instead, you type something along the lines of "make me a website" in the prompt box.

To make a website, you need sources, so I set Gemini Notebook to collect information about James Joyce's 1914 collection of short stories, Dubliners. As I studied Dubliners many years ago (I highly recommend it), I was hoping I'd spot any AI errors and hallucinations, and be able to properly assess the quality of Gemini Notebook's analysis.

With a suitable amount of information absorbed into the AI brain, I got Gemini Notebook to make me a website. Google doesn't give too much official guidance here, but says you should add "a description of what you want to build for work or study," so if you have a particular style or angle in mind, include it.

Here was my prompt: "Build a website using all the sources and information collected so far. Make it an interactive guide to the individual stories in Dubliners, as well as the overarching themes and context. I want to use it as a study aid to explore different aspects of the story collection."

The finished website pops up in the Studio panel, and in my case it took around five minutes to put something together from 26 different sources. Click on the website, and it opens in a new window—you can use it from there, open it in a separate tab, or download the code to your computer.

I was honestly impressed by how much Gemini Notebook managed to pack into a single website: I got plot summaries, analysis, critical context, and some of the main themes and motifs outlined. There was an interactive quiz at the bottom of the page to test my knowledge, and even a graphic I could click through to reveal different elements of how the stories fit together.

Everything was well laid out and functional, albeit with the standard "sheen" you get from anything made by AI (which tends to always pick the same colors and fonts). I wouldn't have necessarily thought that I needed a website if I was still studying Dubliners today, but I can definitely see how it would have helped.

For something like my project, a website really works well as a study aid, since it offers a different way of interrogating the information. You can already do that in the main chat, and with tools such as the audio and video overviews, but this is different. It's a bit like having a mini-Wikipedia focused just on whatever you're researching.

The interactive elements that are included—so in my example, I could click from story to story, and theme to theme, within the same page—make a site more engaging than a standard report document and a static wall of text, and should help the information stick if you're trying to study it.

I can also see how this might be useful for sharing data with others. Maybe you need to analyze monthly report figures, or you want to chart how your organization has developed a product over time—perhaps an interactive website will relay that information in a more compelling way than a slide deck or PDF.

Maybe you have a notebook stuffed with details of a big family vacation. Putting together a website through Gemini Notebook would give your family a hub that they could use to easily check up on the details of the trip—where you're going and when, what you'll see there, what they need to pack, and so on. Another idea might be a family tree, if you have ancestry data and sources packed together in a notebook. Putting together a website would be a great way to visualize it, and, again, it's something you could share with relatives. If there were updates to the data you've collected, you could generate a new website.

Bear in mind that you can do a lot with your prompt. My website turned out the way it did because that's what I asked for, and if you need more interactivity or specific types of sections, then you can ask. Based on my follow-up prompts, you can also specify colors and certain layout choices, though it's not quite a full-scale website builder.

It looks like these sites are single-page creations, though they do have interactive elements and sections that change as you click around. It might be better to think of them as mini apps rather than sites. As of yet, there's no built-in option for publishing a site online or sharing it with others, so you need to download the HTML and do that manually.

Persona 6 Doesn't Have A Date Yet, But Atlus Says You Won't Have To Wait As Long For Persona 7

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There's been a lot of talk around how long GTA fans have been waiting for the latest entry to launch, but Persona isn't actually that far behind. Persona 5 – in its original state – released over a decade ago on 15th September 2016, and although Persona 6 has now been officially announced, we're none the wiser as to when it will actually come out.

It's likely to finally land at some point in 2027 after the dust has settled from Persona 4 Revival, but according to a recent interview with general producer Kazuhisa Wada (thanks, Nintendo Everything), fans won't have to wait quite so long for the inevitable Persona 7.

"We will continue to cherish what makes Persona unique, while also embracing changes in the times and needs, and taking on new challenges. We aim to be a brand that can grow for 5, 10 years, and beyond.

"Among those projects, the one we’re currently working on is Persona 6. While there’s still limited information I can share, we want to prove the results of all the challenges we’ve faced so far through the game itself. With that in mind, we’re creating something even better than before, and we’re feeling a real sense of accomplishment as we work on it.

"And Persona 6 is not the end. We are working on various things to ensure that you don’t have to wait as long for the next installment as we have in the past. And in order to make Persona an even bigger IP, we are constantly thinking about creative ideas and developments that will not disappoint your expectations. Please look forward to what’s to come from Persona.”

We will look forward to it, Wada-san, thank you sir. Let's keep the focus on Persona 4 Revival and Persona 6 for now though, hm?

It's not a surprising statement, mind you. Game development is taking longer than ever these days, which also makes it more expensive than ever. So a lot of publishers are looking into potential solutions to cut down on dev time and increase the cadence of releases. As long as it doesn't involve handing everything off to AI, we're fine with that.

Persona 4 Revival is currently scheduled to launch on Switch 2 on 20th May 2027, a few months after other platforms. As for Persona 6, an imminent reveal has been rumoured recently thanks to a backend update on the official website. Hardly concrete, but it's a start.

Cell to Singularity's Future update takes you into space for a new look at humanity's progress

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There's an odd fascination amongst developers for trying to simulate the progression of humanity. Surviving Mars, Alpha Centauri and, of course, Civilization. But in Cell to Singularity, developer Computer Lunch has been working towards a simulation of everything, with the latest major step being their Future update.

Cell to Singularity is, essentially, exactly what it says on the tin. Your goal is to explore and guide a simulation of an entire human civilisation, starting from simple single-celled organisms all the way up to the space-faring apes we all know and love. And the Future expansion, naturally, takes you into the speculative future of space travel and beyond.

That includes elements ranging from space tourism to artificial general intelligence and beyond. All of which it's up to you to guide in different directions, seeing humanity change and grow as they go beyond orbit and away to the moon and beyond, establishing a permanent presence away from the planet.

The Future expansion doesn't only introduce grand-scale changes like this, but also more tweaks to fundamental progression. You'll have access to the new Prediction Matrix to boost your progress, as well as a revamped Mission System and more. All this, and more music too! The Future, it seems, has never looked brighter for fans of Cell to Singularity.

But if you'd prefer your strategy a little more grounded, without the pressure of guiding humanity from cradle to space, then we've got more options for you. Just take a look at our list of the best strategy games on Android for some of our choice picks!

Peak Halftime Show: The A-List Machine Eventizing Pro Games

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

No longer content with just a Super Bowl splash, pro leagues led by the NFL are increasingly booking major music stars for programming. But it’s a balancing act.

By Alex Weprin, Ethan Millman November 20, 2026 8:56am

Nearly 73,000 fans inside Rio De Janeiro’s Maracanã Stadium rocked out to Ricky Martin and Brazilian DJ Pedro Sampaio on Sept. 27, when the duo performed their song “pikito pikito” live for the first time. But they weren’t headlining a concert — they were playing a halftime show.

The NFL has long held global ambitions, but this is the year it leaned into music to serve as a sort of cultural diplomacy. The Rio game, between the Baltimore Ravens and Dallas Cowboys, may be the best example, combining a global superstar in Martin with a local talent. “There was a lot of preparation and many hours of rehearsals leading up to the performance,” Martin tells The Hollywood Reporter, “but it was one of those experiences where everything came together beautifully.”

He adds, “These are memories I’ll carry with me for a long time.”

In fact, the NFL has seven halftime shows planned this season, kicking off with the Jonas Brothers in Melbourne on Sept. 10, running through The Warning in Mexico City on Nov. 22. It’s also part of a concerted effort to elevate games into cultural events. Just look at Nov. 25, when U2 will perform during a halftime show for Netflix’s Thanksgiving Eve game at SoFi Stadium between the L.A. Rams and Green Bay Packers. “There’s really this gathering momentum about how we can further eventize some of the parts on our schedule and our calendar,” Hans Schroeder, executive vp and chief media officer of the NFL, told reporters in October.

The league is leaning into halftime shows as the sports world overall has been more heavily embracing music programming in games. FIFA for instance, had its first World Cup Final halftime show this summer, with Coldplay’s Chris Martin curating a lineup that included BTS, Madonna and Justin Bieber. The NCAA, meanwhile, tapped EDM act The Chainsmokers to play a “bridge show” between the two Final Four games. The NHL has dug in, too, tapping Benson Boone to headline the first intermission during this year’s Winter Classic on New Year’s Eve.

“The NFL and all these leagues understand that to grow the game internationally and even domestically for the younger generation, they need to find organic ways to get those eyeballs,” says Tyler Stimmel, a booking agent in UTA’s music brand partnerships division. He handled booking on many of the NFL’s international events this year, including Jonas Brothers, Burna Boy in Paris, The Warning and Drew Baldridge in Munich.

“It all stems from the younger generation,” Stimmel says, pointing toward a recent USC Annenberg study that suggested nearly 80 percent of Gen Z respondents in a survey suggested they’d go to a sporting event because of concerts and other brand activations rather than because of the game itself. It’s beneficial to the talent, too, with international acts getting a shot to play before a wide swath of stateside NFL fans who otherwise likely wouldn’t have been exposed to their music. The agent adds, “We live in a highly saturated pop culture ecosystem the NFL wants more ways to cut through. “Music is the most natural way because we already know where to put the show, and you can pick a booking to tap into a version of culture that people within the region can easily relate to.”

In the U.S., that means working with such partners as Netflix to create more event-sized moments, like having U2 perform. “Netflix has leaned in because they see the success of Super Bowl and the Super Bowl halftime show, and it draws more eyes and it brings more attention and it brings more people, and not necessarily all avid football fans,” says Jon Barker, senior VP of global event production and operations for the NFL. “Now we’re tapping into casual fans, now we’re tapping into new fans, and it’s just one more element that opens another door for somebody to walk into the ecosystem of football and participate.”

Unlike the Super Bowl, which is booked by Jay-Z’s Roc Nation (next year’s headliner is Jay-Z himself), the league books its other halftime shows itself, though Barker notes that there is close coordination with its media rights partners. “It gets a little complicated, to be honest with you, in trying to find the right balance,” Barker says of the booking process. “Who is touring and is available? Who can put on a show that can fill a stadium? Who will translate well and do well on television? Who is the right act for the audience that’s inside this area, and who is the right act that will attract new fans that will be interested in what we’re doing?”

Stimmel, for his part, describes the process as sophisticated, saying the NFL has heavily relied on its regional offices throughout the world, identifying artists who are a major draw in each market but who can pull viewers from several verticals. “Burna Boy in Paris in particular is one that checks off a lot of those boxes,” he says. “He was one of the first Nigerian artists to sell out Stade de France, so we know he is popular there, but he can draw from Africa, North America.”

While the increased bookings may bring in more younger viewers, they may not always be a popular offering to older fans, who might prefer to see just a traditional halftime report with pundits rather than a concert. Stimmel acknowledges that critique but adds that “they want this to appeal to everyone, but the leagues are recognizing that in order to sustain the sport, it has to appeal to younger fans.”

NFL exec Barker says the league isn’t done expanding its halftime ambitions, noting that while “there’s probably a limit and the right balance, I don’t think we’re there yet,” even if it maintains the Super Bowl as the ultimate showcase.

From the league’s point of view, the performances are ultimately about growing the game — and reasserting the league as the most culturally relevant piece of American pop culture. “The way that we think about music is the same way we think about art, the same way we think about fashion,” says Barker, adding that the more ways “we can open to bring somebody in and show them the sport, the better off we all are.”
